After the king, who was to rule according to the established tradition, went to the forest to perform penance, the ministers, who were wise and learned in statecraft, deliberated for some days and then, following the ancient tradition, went to our Nayanar, who was engaged in sacred service at Tiruvanchaikkalam, the sacred place of the moon-crowned Lord. The ministers, who were sharp-witted, deliberated for some days after the king, who was to rule according to the established royal lineage, went to the forest to perform penance as mentioned before. They studied all the texts pertaining to the succession of kingship according to the royal tradition and understood the matter clearly. Having deliberated and understood, they went to Tiruvanchaikkalam and approached our Nayanar, who was performing sacred service there. The 12th sacred verse. Having approached him, they bowed before him and said, "The right to rule this mountainous country, which is filled with waterfalls, has come to you by the traditional law of succession, so please accept the crown without blemish." Thus spoke the truthful ministers, praising him. The ministers went before our Nayanar, bowed, and submitted their request. They said, "The right to rule this mountainous country, which is filled with waterfalls, has come to you by the traditional law of succession, so please accept the crown without blemish." They stood praising him. The custom of the mountainous country, the law of the mountainous country, is the traditional way of succession, where the right to inherit comes through the maternal line. This is also known as Marumakkathayam. In Tamil Nadu, in other kingdoms like those of the Cholas and Pandyas, the male line of succession, where the son inherits from the father, is followed. But in the mountainous country of the Cheras, the tradition is that the succession comes through the female line, through the sister. We can still see this in the state of Kerala today. We learn about this in their laws. It is because of this system of Marumakkathayam that we see women having more rights and privileges in this mountainous country of Kerala. We also see the name Pen Malayala (Female Kerala) being used. Thus, the ministers stood submitting their request. It is customary to praise the king or anyone to whom a request is being made before making the request. Here, the Nayanar is not only a king but also a great person who is deeply devoted to Lord Shiva. Therefore, it was necessary to praise him. Accordingly, they made their request, praising him. The 13th sacred verse. "If there is grace to rule without deviation from the state of love, even though this is an impediment to the sacred service that brings increasing joy, I will take leave from the Lord who wears bones and snakes, and I will know His will," said the Nayanar, bowing before the Lord and submitting his request with the grace of the Lord. Our Nayanar, Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, felt in his heart that these ministers were making such a request, which was an impediment to his sacred service that brought ever-increasing joy. He thought, "If there is grace from the Lord to rule without deviating from the state of love for Shiva, I will take leave from the Lord and know His will." He entered the temple, bowed before the Lord, and submitted his request. The intention of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar was that the ministers were requesting him to accept the kingship, which was an impediment to his sacred service to Lord Shiva. This was the Nayanar's inner feeling. Although he was born with the right to rule, our Nayanar did not desire those rights and worldly pleasures. From a young age, he did not stay in the palace in Kodungolur but stayed in Tiruvanchaikkalam and performed sacred services to Lord Shiva, as we have seen. Now, even though he was offered the kingship, the Nayanar's heart remained in its previous state. It is the nature of Shiva's devotees to despise worldly pleasures such as kingship and to dedicate themselves to praising the Lord's feet and serving His devotees. The Tiruvachakam states, "Bhogam Vendi Vendilen Purandara Inbamum," meaning, "I do not desire pleasures, nor do I desire worldly happiness." The 11th Tirumurai Tiruvidaimarudur Mummanikkovai states, "Indirac Chelvamum Ettu Siththiyum Vanthuli Vanthuli Marutthanan Odungi," meaning, "When the wealth of Indra and the eight siddhis came, I rejected them and moved away." The Periyapuranam states, "Koodum Anbinil Kumbidalai Anri Veendum Venda Viralil Vilanginar," meaning, "They shone in their desire not for liberation, but for bowing with increasing love." We see countless such sacred sayings. We see the nature of Shiva's devotees in this way. Therefore, he thought, "If there is grace from the Lord to rule without deviating from the state of love for Shiva, I will take leave from the Lord and know His will." He decided to submit his request to the Lord and know His will. There are specific times and places for submitting requests to the Lord, according to the rules of Shiva worship. The rules of Shiva worship and temple visitation also state this. The Nayanar's inner desire was that service to the Lord is the sacred service that brings increasing joy. Therefore, it is understood that the nature of kingship is not of that kind. Joy comes from being immersed in the grace of Shiva. That joy is born of love. We have seen that the Lord Himself declared the nature of devotees as those who find joy in love. We have seen that the Mother requested "iravatha inba anbu" (undying joyful love). The Tiruvachakam states, "Inbame Ennudaiya Anbe," meaning, "Joy is my love." We will see that Aiyadigal Kadavarkon Nayanar, despising kingship as a source of trouble, entrusted it to his beautiful son and became one who delighted in performing sacred service. Therefore, the Nayanar requested that if he were to rule, it should be with the grace that would allow him to do so without deviating from the state of love for Shiva. Indeed, the Nayanar's heart believed that royal pleasures and worldly pleasures are not permanent. If so, why did he not immediately refuse the ministers' request? Why did he not refuse immediately, but instead went to the Lord and submitted his request to know His will? This is because the Nayanar realized that it was the Lord's grace that had guided him to Shiva's service and would continue to do so without fail. However, the Nayanar also realized that it was the Lord's grace that had placed him in the royal lineage, brought the kingship to him through tradition, and thereby placed these worldly duties upon him. Therefore, he also thought that he should not refuse these duties. In the Kambar Ramayanam, we see Kambar stating that Sri Rama understood that the responsibility of kingship was a duty. Therefore, he wondered whether to continue with "En Kadan Pani Seithu Kidappathe" (My duty is to serve and remain) or to accept the kingship that had come by grace without opposing it and perform worldly duties according to the Lord's grace. He was in doubt as to what was to be done. He feared accepting the kingship because it would be an impediment to his sacred service. At the same time, caste, lifespan, and pleasures are the results of past karma. Therefore, refusing those duties would be acting against divine grace. Therefore, he also feared refusing the kingship that had come by grace. He also feared accepting it. The Nayanar arose, saying, "If divine grace commands me to rule without deviating from the state of love, I will submit my request to the Lord and know His will." Here, we should reflect on the Tiruvachakam, "Vendathakkathu Arivoy Nee Venda Muzhuvathum Thruvoy Nee Vendi Niyatha Arul Seithai Yanum Athuve Vendinallal Vendum Parisu Onru Undu Ennil Athuvum Ninthan Viruppanre," meaning, "You know what is to be desired, you give everything that is desired, you have bestowed grace without my asking, I too desire only that, if there is anything else to be desired, that too is your will." We have read about the history of Tirugnanasambandar Peruman submitting his request to know the Lord's will before going to debate with the Jains at Madurai Tirualavayil. We see Tirunavukkarasu Peruman submitting his request, "Ponnar Tiruvadikku Onru Undu Vinnappam Potri Seyyum En Avi Kappatharku Ichai Undel," meaning, "I have a request for your golden feet, if you wish to protect my soul." Therefore, even if divine grace led him to accept the duty of kingship, the Nayanar's intention was that it should be without deviating from his love for the Lord. Therefore, he submits his request to the Lord in this manner. Our Nayanar submits his request, asking what the message of divine grace is, what the command of divine grace is. Let us continue to reflect. Tiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 05 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Tiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 941, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. The Nayanar submits his request to the Lord, asking for the command of divine grace. The 14th sacred verse. "Having received the right to rule, and with boundless love and devotion, he received the understanding to know all that everyone speaks, and also received endless courage, unfailing generosity, and all the qualities proper to a king, such as an army and vehicles." By the grace of the Lord, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar received the Lord's command. Our Nayanar understood that accepting the right to rule and remaining in the path of love was the Lord's grace. He had requested to rule without deviating from his love for the Lord. The Lord granted him exactly what he desired. In addition, the Lord Himself bestowed upon the Nayanar the necessary components for such a rule. Kings are not just rulers for the people under them. They have the responsibility to protect all other living beings. Therefore, kings have the responsibility to protect non-human beings as well as humans. Only if they have the understanding to know the languages spoken by all those beings can they protect non-human beings. Therefore, the Lord bestowed upon our Nayanar the understanding to know the languages and feelings of all people and all beings. In addition, this Nayanar was in a state of Sivayoga. For those who have attained Sivayoga, the sounds of all beings are contained within the Nada sound that is perceived. We have already seen the responsibility of kings to protect all living beings, including people, in the section on the greatness of the city. Therefore, our Nayanar was in such a state. That is why our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar chanted the Shiva mantra into his horse's ear, mounted it, and reached Tirukkailayam, as we will see in that history. Therefore, it was because he had the understanding to know the languages and feelings of all humans and all living beings that this Nayanar, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, was given the sacred name Kazharitrarivar. The 15th sacred verse. "Having received that grace, he bowed and came out, and though ruling would be a blemish to his sacred service, by the grace of the Lord, he agreed to the ministers who had requested him to wear the glorious crown, and the worthy ministers bowed and joyfully undertook that task." As mentioned before, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, having received the divine grace, bowed and came out of the temple. It is his duty to follow the path shown by grace. Therefore, even though ruling would be an impediment to his sacred service, having received the Lord's grace, he gave his consent to the ministers who had requested him to wear the glorious crown. Those ministers bowed at the Nayanar's feet and joyfully undertook the desired tasks. The 16th sacred verse. "On the auspicious day and hour of his coronation, with all the necessary and glorious auspicious items arranged, and all the auspicious rituals performed, Kazharitrarivar, who understood the nature of righteous justice that brings joy to all living beings, crowned himself with a single crown that was suitable for both worlds." This is the sacred verse. On that auspicious day, at that auspicious hour destined for coronation, with all the special auspicious items arranged and all the auspicious rituals performed, the Nayanar crowned himself. He is Kazharitrarivar, who protects this world by understanding the nature of righteous justice that brings joy to all living beings, classified as high-caste and low-caste. He crowned himself with a single crown that was suitable for both this world and the next. The 17th sacred verse. "Having circumambulated the Lord's temple and bowed before Him, he mounted the mighty elephant, and with the white umbrella and fly-whisk held by his loyal attendants, he circumambulated the beautiful city, and then he saw a washerman in front of him, carrying a bundle of alkaline earth on his shoulder." Having circumambulated the Lord's temple and bowed before Him, touching the ground, he mounted the mighty elephant. With the white umbrella and fly-whisk held by his attendants, he circumambulated the city. Therefore, we see that the coronation took place in the palace where the Nayanar resided in Tiruvanchaikkalam. Because the divine Sekkizhar Peruman sings that immediately after the coronation, he circumambulated the Lord's temple and bowed before Him, we can understand that the coronation ceremony took place in the palace where the Nayanar resided in Tiruvanchaikkalam. Immediately after the coronation, he went to the temple, worshipped the Lord, and then set out. This period, that is, before he accepted the kingship, the Nayanar had established a separate monastery in Tiruvanchaikkalam and resided there, performing all the temple services. That place is still there today, a short distance south of the temple (which people now call Tiruvanchikulam). The researcher of the Kochi government discovered it and erected a memorial pillar there, inscribing it as the place where Cheraman Perumal Nayanar's palace was located. They have written it as "The site of the palace of Sri Saint Cheraman." Four Shiva lingams are also embedded there. Even today, the Kochi kings, after their coronation, first worship at Tiruvanchaikkalam and then present an elephant as a special custom, as written by Sivakavimani Aiya. We see that the custom of the king riding an elephant and circumambulating the city is still prevalent in the mountainous country of Kochi and Travancore. We also see the tradition of holding umbrellas and fly-whisks over the elephant. It is customary for the crowned king to circumambulate the city in the same attire. Now, our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, after his coronation, worshipped at the Tiruvanchaikkalam Appar temple and then set out from there on an elephant in the same attire to Kodungolur, the capital. This is the city procession. As he was proceeding, he saw a washerman carrying a bundle of alkaline earth on his shoulder. The 18th sacred verse. "Seeing the washerman, whose body was whitened by the alkaline earth that had dissolved in the rain, as a devotee of the Lord with the sacred hand, he dismounted from the elephant with the excellent forehead ornament and quickly went and bowed to him with overflowing love." We saw that the washerman was carrying a bundle of alkaline earth. Due to the rain, the alkaline earth dissolved and seeped, making the washerman's body appear white. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, realizing that this was the sacred attire of a devotee of the Lord, dismounted from the elephant he was riding. He dismounted from the elephant's neck and quickly went forward with great love and bowed to the washerman with folded hands. The alkaline earth carried by the washerman had dissolved in the rain and seeped, making his body appear white. Even today, we see that it rains many times in a single day during the rainy season in that country. The appearance of whiteness on the body reminded Cheraman Perumal Nayanar of the sacred attire of a devotee of Shiva. That was the Nayanar's state of maturity. The sacred attire of a devotee is determined by the brilliance of the sacred ash. We saw this in the history of Enathinatha Nayanar. We also saw the Puranam of Meypporul Nayanar, who considered even cunning people who smeared sacred ash all over their bodies as his own. We also saw in the Puranam of Siruttonda Nayanar that when no devotee was found to feed the Bhairava devotee, he requested to be accepted as a devotee of Shiva solely for having applied sacred ash. Therefore, the whiteness on the washerman's body was not due to sacred ash. Nevertheless, it reminded the Nayanar of sacred ash and showed him the sacred attire of a devotee. Even today, there are people who, upon seeing the whiteness of sacred ash, are reminded of the whiteness of lime. Therefore, immediately upon seeing the sacred attire of the devotee, bowing with love is a sign of true devotion, as stated in Sivagnanabodham, "Vedamum Aran ena Thozhume," meaning, "Even the Vedas worship it as Shiva." Immediately upon seeing the sacred attire of the devotee, he quickly dismounted from the elephant's neck with love and bowed to him. It is forbidden to bow while seated on a vehicle. Therefore, he quickly dismounted and went quickly to the washerman and bowed to him with folded hands. This shows that the Nayanar was always devoted to Shiva worship, without giving any thought to his worldly status as a king, or that he was crowned, or that he was making a city procession, or that he was riding on an elephant. Thus, the Nayanar bowed to the washerman. The 19th sacred verse. "Seeing the Chera king bowing to him, the washerman's mind was disturbed, and he bowed before him and said, 'Who do you take me to be? I am Adi Vannaan.' The Chera king replied, 'I am Adi Cheran. You have reminded me of the sacred attire of sacred ash. Go without worry.'" Seeing the Chera king bowing to him, the washerman's mind was disturbed, and he bowed before him and said, "O King, who do you take me to be, that you do this? I am Adi Vannaan, your servant, the washerman." He thought that the king had mistakenly taken him for someone else and bowed, and so he revealed the truth. Therefore, the washerman's tact in pointing out the king's mistake, his humility in stating his true status, and his noble mindset and culture are indicated. This indicates the high worldly civilization of the Tamils in those days. To that washerman who spoke thus, our Nayanar, Cheraman Peruman, said, "I am Adi Cheran." This place fills us with wonder at the Nayanar's exalted love, his high worldly civilization, and his timely and sweet words. To his statement, "I am Adi Vannaan," the king replied, "I am Adi Cheran." Cheraman Perumal Nayanar continued to say to the washerman, "You have reminded me of the sacred attire, which is the brilliance of sacred ash. Therefore, you may go without worry." Therefore, he means, "Do not think in any way that you have caused an interruption in the king's city procession or that you have become the cause of any untoward incident. Continue on your way." Thus, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar replied. Seeing the king's sacred service, all the ministers folded their hands above their heads and praised him. Let us continue to reflect. Tiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 06 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Tiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 942, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. The 20th sacred verse. Seeing the king's sacred service, all the wise ministers bowed with folded hands above their heads and praised him. The king mounted the mighty elephant and entered the gate of the palace, adorned with golden jewels, after circumambulating the ancient city with its streets filled with mansions adorned with flags, so that the auspicious decorations might shine. Seeing the king's sacred service, all the wise ministers bowed with folded hands above their heads and praised him. The king mounted the mighty elephant and entered the gate of the palace, adorned with golden jewels, after circumambulating the ancient city with its streets filled with mansions adorned with flags. Flags are essential features of all great cities. Kodungolur is such a city, filled with mansions. The king, after circumambulating that city, arrives at the gate of his palace, which is located in Kodungolur. Since the crowned king is arriving, the people welcome him grandly with decorations and auspicious items such as full pots, incense, and lamps. The 21st sacred verse. "Having dismounted from the elephant, he ascended the exalted throne in the jeweled hall, and as the white umbrella cast its shade above him, and the beautiful-eyed women waved white fly-whisks before him, and many flowers were scattered, the great king, the Nayanar, sat enthroned, praised by the mighty kings." Having dismounted from the elephant, he ascended the exalted throne in the jeweled hall. As the white umbrella cast its shade above him, and the women waved white fly-whisks before him, and many flowers were scattered, the great king, the Nayanar, sat enthroned, praised by the mighty kings. All the tributary kings bowed, scattering flowers and praising him. Thus, the great king sat enthroned, praised by the tributary kings. The 22nd sacred verse. "He ruled the earth, protecting the world, with the generous Cholas and the rightful Pandyas, as great three kings, upholding justice according to the Manusmriti, bringing tribute from countless kings, eradicating internal and external enemies, and fostering the radiant light of sacred ash and the growth of the Vedas." This sacred verse describes how our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar ruled the earth, protecting the world. He ruled alongside the generous Cholas, who protected the world, and the rightful Pandyas, as great three kings. He upheld justice according to the Manusmriti, received tribute from countless kings, eradicated internal and external enemies, and fostered the radiant light of sacred ash and the growth of the Vedas. The crowned kings, the three Tamil kings, are the Cheras, Cholas, and Pandyas. These royal lineages have existed since the time of creation, according to tradition. These rulers ruled over the entire Tamil Nadu. At times, their rule extended up to the Himalayas and even across the seas. Therefore, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman praises them as rulers who protected the world and whose rule was eternal, stating that these three lineages ruled the world as crowned kings. "Valavar" refers to the Cholas. "Chezhiyar" refers to the Pandyas. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman refers to the Chola kings as "Ulaku Purakkum Kodai Valavar" (generous Cholas who protect the world). The Chola lineage was a dynasty that followed the Saiva path without deviation. The Chola lineage was also characterized by its ability to establish this world without deviation from that path. Generosity is that which brings both spiritual and physical well-being to life. Such generous people are the "Valavars," the wealthy ones. They are the Chola kings. Now, when referring to the Pandyas, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman refers to them as "Urimai Chezhiyar" (rightful Pandyas). This indicates that they were the rightful ones, the lineage that intermarried with the Cholas. We will see in this history that during the time of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, when the Chola king, who had married the daughter of the Pandya king, was staying in Madurai, the Chola and Pandya kings met and rejoiced with our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and Sundaramurthy Swamigal when they came to Madurai. In addition, we will see the history of these three kings coming together and worshipping at many sacred places. Therefore, we see that during this period, these three kings were in friendship, without internal conflicts or other differences. We also see that the tributary kings and petty chieftains under our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, who was a great emperor, paid their respective tributes. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar ruled by eradicating internal and external enemies. External enemies are those who are hostile and unfriendly kings. Internal enemies are those who come from within oneself, from one's own people, from thieves, from living beings, and from oneself. Our Nayanar ruled by establishing the submissive kings who paid tribute on the right path and by punishing and removing the disobedient ones. We see that in those days, there were no governmental enmities such as anarchy, treason, various parties, or factions among the people of Tamil Nadu, which we see today. This is one meaning of internal and external enemies. Now, the inner meaning is that internal enemies are those that arise from ignorance, lust, and other evils, and external enemies are those that arise from the five senses. He ruled by establishing the people on the right path through good conduct, good teachings, and other means, so that they would not suffer from internal enemies such as ignorance and lust, and by removing the evils that come from the five senses, which are external enemies, and by making the people free from ego and attachment, he ruled. Therefore, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman sings that he ruled this world, eradicating these internal and external enemies, fostering the radiant light of sacred ash, and the growth of the Vedas. As per "Veda Neri Thazhaithonga Migu Saivathurai Vilanga" (May the Vedic path flourish and the great Saiva path shine), our king ruled so that Vedic disciplines and Saiva Agamic disciplines would prevail well. Let us continue to reflect. Tiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 07 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. The Periya Puranam, Part 943, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. The 23rd verse. The Chera king, Perumal, with an intellect that clearly understood that the benefits derived from a long-standing great kingdom, abundant penance, sought-after wealth, and great support were none other than the dancing foot in Thillai Thiru Chidambaram, undertook the worship of that raised sacred foot with overflowing love. The right to rule a great kingdom for the Cheras was a long-standing right. It was a right passed down through generations since the beginning of creation. It was the state of being a crowned king. In the previous verse, we saw the benefits that accrue to this world and its people, protected by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, such as administering justice, eradicating enmity, fostering the light of sacred ash, and promoting the Vedas. These are the benefits that accrue to him as a king who protects the land. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman refers to this as "the benefits derived from a great kingdom." The Chera king, Perumal, worships with overflowing love, believing that the benefits derived from a great kingdom, along with abundant penance, sought-after wealth, and great support, are the raised golden foot that dances in Thillai Chidambaram. The benefits derived from a great kingdom are the benefits he receives from ruling. Abundant penance means that only if one has performed penance before will they gain the right to rule. If that penance is lacking, then that is why this earthly birth and worldly pleasures were granted. To make that penance abundant is to make all these pleasures that have been obtained subservient to the Lord and a means to attain liberation. Making all these pleasures a means to attain liberation is the benefit of ruling a great kingdom. That is abundant penance. Now, the sought-after wealth. That is the enduring wealth attained through this penance. Now, great support. All other forms of support, such as material and living beings, will perish without support. In this state where these souls are bound, our Lord is the eternal companion who never leaves them through countless births. Moreover, in the state of liberation, He is the one who grants unending and imperishable joy by keeping them under His sacred feet. Therefore, whether it is in the state of isolation, the state of involvement, or the state of liberation, our Lord, the great dancer who always dances in Thillai Thiru Chidambaram, is always the companion and great support. The sacred words of Thevaram state this as "Perumthunai Perumattra Puliyooranai." Therefore, our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar undertook the worship, believing that the benefits derived from this great kingdom, abundant penance, sought-after wealth, and great support were that dancing anklet, that raised sacred foot, in Thillai Chidambaram. The dancing anklet here refers to the act of grace, which is one of the five divine functions. That raised sacred foot is called the Kunchitha Padam. The raised sacred foot means the foot that lifts souls from the pit of defilement, from the attachment to defilement. We saw the details of this in the section on "Edakkum Maakkathai." Our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar performed worship of that sacred foot, which lifts souls from the bondage of attachment, due to his overflowing love.

The divine Sekkizhar Peruman shows us what kind of worship he performed in the next 24th verse. "Vasa Thirumanjanam Pallithamam Santham Manithupam Thesil Perugum Sezhum Deepam Muthalayinavum Thiru Amuthum Isarkku Ettra Parisinal Archithu Arula Ennalum Pusaikku Amarntha Perumkoothar Porpa Silambin Oli Alithar" is the verse. Our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar performs worship by offering fragrant sacred ablution, sacred garlands, sandalwood paste, beautiful incense, brightly burning lamps, and other offerings, as well as sacred food, in a manner befitting the Lord, as prescribed. All these mentioned in this verse are the materials and components for the external worship of the Lord. Thirumanjanam means sacred ablution. It is fragrant sacred ablution. This verse mentions "Vasa Thirumanjanam Pallithamam Santha Manithupam." The word "Vasam" (fragrance) that comes first can be interpreted as applying to each item. Therefore, "Vasa Thirumanjanam" means it is customary to add fragrant flowers, green camphor, saffron, and other fragrant ingredients to the sacred ablution water to make it fragrant. This "Manjanam" is called sacred bath or Abhishekam. Therefore, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar performs this Thirumanjanam for our Lord, the great dancer. Now, the garland, which is Thirupallithamam, and sandalwood paste, which is Santham. The previously mentioned Manjanam refers to Abhishekam, and now the garland, Thirupallithamam, and sandalwood refer to decoration. Now, Manithupam and Deepam are mentioned. These are types of offerings. Thesil Deepam, Thesu means light. It also means knowledge. Therefore, brightly burning lamps are also a part of the worship performed by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. The sacred words of Thevaram state, "Vilakittar Perusollin Meyneri Gnanamagum." Now, he also offers sacred food. Therefore, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar performs this worship with all these items and components. How does he do it? This verse states, "Isarkku Ettra Parisinal Archithu." "Isarkku Ettra Parisu" means in a manner befitting the Lord, that is, according to the Sivagamas, which prescribe what is suitable for the worship of Shiva, the five offerings that are part of Shiva worship are mentioned here. The five offerings are those related to earth. They are called Parthivopacharas. They include sacred garlands, sandalwood paste, and sacred food. Now, the offerings related to water. They are called Jalopacharas. They include sacred ablution, milk, and tender coconut water. Now, the offerings related to fire. They are called Taijasopacharas. They include lamps, camphor, gold, and jeweled ornaments. Fourthly, the offerings related to air. They are called Vayavyopacharas. They include incense and chamara (fly-whisk). Now, the offerings related to light. They are called Akasopacharas. They include bells, musical instruments, and praises. With these, he worships, adores, and performs puja. He performs this worship every day. While he was performing puja, the Lord, the great dancer, who had graciously appeared for the puja, allowed Cheraman Perumal Nayanar to hear the sound of His beautiful anklets. This Lord Nataraja, worshipped by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, who graciously made the sound of His anklets audible to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar daily during puja, is still worshipped today in the Thiru Anjaikkalam temple. On His pedestal, the name "Thiru Anjaikkalam Sabapathi" is inscribed in Tamil.

We can still see it today. Lord Nataraja, who blessed him by making the anklets sound. It was the sound of the Nada anklets, audible to Shiva yogis. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar was proficient in Shiva yoga. Therefore, the sound of anklets is one of the ten types of sounds heard in that yoga. The great dancer makes that sound audible to the Nayanar. The 25th verse. "Nambar Thalinil Vazhipattu Nalil Inbamutru Amarkindrar. Imbar Ulakil Iravalarkkum Variyor Evarkkum Eegaiyinal Sempon Mazhaiyam Ena Pozhinthu Thirunthu Vetriyudan Porunthi Umbar Potra Tham Perumanukku Uriya Velvi Pala Seithar." The Nayanar, who daily enjoyed bliss by worshipping the Lord's sacred feet, our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, showered red gold like rain upon all who came to him begging and upon the poor in this world, as they desired, and performed many sacrifices befitting his Lord, praised by the celestials, with perfect victory. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman first sang about the Nayanar's state of ruling and protecting this world. Then, in the next two verses, he described how the Nayanar performed that rule without hindrance to his divine service. In this verse, he describes the joy that he and the world received as a result of those states. Not only the earthly world but also the celestial world enjoyed bliss due to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar's rule, which brought daily joy through daily worship. It is true that the celestials are pleased by the sacrifices performed in this world. "Sirappodu Pusanai Sellathu Vanam Varakumel Vanorkkum Eendu" is a Kural. Therefore, the celestials are the beneficiaries of the sacrifices performed in this world. They participate in these sacrifices, receive their share of the offerings, and bestow necessary benefits upon the people of this world, as commanded by the Lord. Thus, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar's rule brought good not only to the people of this world but also to the celestials. Our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar showers upon those who come to him begging whatever they need, like rain. He also seeks out and gives charity to those who suffer from poverty without asking him. He blesses them like rain. He carries out his rule successfully without any hindrance. This shows the valor, greatness, and skill of his rule. The victories achieved by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar were not due to a desire for land. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar's victories occurred because he wanted to reform the people of those countries and keep them on the right path. Therefore, it is clear to us that these were not conflicts driven by greed. Thus, our Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, after ruling well, also performed the Shiva sacrifices mentioned in the Sivagamas very excellently. All these acts of charity are essential qualities for a king. Therefore, our Nayanar prayed to the Lord and received them. We see that he also ruled accordingly. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. The Periya Puranam, Part 944, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman begins to explain another divine grace, a sacred history, in the sacred puranam of Cheraman Peruman Nayanar. In the Thiruvilayadal Puranam, in the Koodal Kandam, the 41st chapter is "Viragu Vitra Padalam" (The Chapter of Selling Firewood). It is the chapter where Madurai Somasundara Peruman performed the divine play of selling firewood in the streets of Madurai for Panabathirar. That sacred history is known to all of us. Following the divine play of selling firewood for Panabathirar, the next chapter was "Thirumugam Kodutha Padalam" (The Chapter of Giving the Sacred Letter). This divine play was also performed by the Lord for Panabathirar. After the divine play of selling firewood, Panabathirar only performs the worship of the Lord. He does nothing else. Therefore, poverty reaches him. For some days, the Lord brings wealth from the Pandya king's treasury and gives it to Panabathirar. Then, Somasundara Peruman gives Panabathirar a palm-leaf manuscript with a verse written on it, "Mathimali Purisai," and instructs him to take it to the Chera king. That verse is the Thirumuga Pasuram (Sacred Letter Verse). What we now call a letter, when elders send it to younger ones, they call it Srimugam or Thirumugam. Somasundara Peruman himself writes such a Thirumugam and gives it to Panabathirar, asking him to deliver it to the Chera king. Similarly, he informs the Chera king in a dream. The Chera king, understanding this, sends messengers to bring Panabathirar there. He then has the sacred letter given by the Lord and Panabathirar carried on an elephant, parades them through the city, brings them to the palace, treats them well, and happily opens his treasury, telling Panabathirar to take whatever he needs, and sends him off. This is what the Thirumugam Kodutha Padalam Thiruvilayadal Puranam narrates. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman sings about that sacred history here. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman will now sing about the sacred history of Panabathirar coming to the Chera country, to Kodungolur, and upon hearing of it, our Cheraman Peruman Nayanar welcoming him happily, reading the Thirumuga Pasuram given by the Lord with emotion, and giving Panabathirar the gold and wealth he needed. Therefore, this sacred history appears in both the Thiruvilayadal Puranam and the Periya Puranam. It appears with some minor differences. The news found in the Thiruvilayadal Puranam is that the Chera king was informed in a dream and that the Chera king sent messengers to find Panabathirar and bring him here. These are the two places where the Thiruvilayadal Puranam differs from the original work, the Periya Puranam, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Therefore, those minor differences found in the Thiruvilayadal Puranam can be considered as being about courtesy. Now, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman begins to sing this divine play. The 26th verse. "Innavannam Ivar Ozhuka Ezhil Kol Pandinan Nattu Mannum Madurai Thiruvalavayil Iraivar Varum Anbal Pannum Isai Padalil Paravum Pananar Pathiranarukku Nanmai Needu Perum Selvam Nalka Vendi Arul Purivar." While Cheraman Peruman Nayanar was ruling with such excellence, Lord Somasundara, who resides in Thiruvalavai in the beautiful Pandya country, in the established Madurai, desired to bestow great wealth upon Pananar Pathiranar, who praised Him with musical songs, and graciously blessed him. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman here refers to the country as "Pandi Nannattu."

He refers to the city as "Mannum Madurai." He refers to the temple as "Thiru Alavai." The divine Sekkizhar Peruman thus refers to the Lord, Somasundara Kadavul, who resides in that temple. Somasundara Peruman desired to bless Panabathirar. He desired to bestow wealth that would bring long-lasting well-being and greatness. This wealth comes from the Lord's grace. Therefore, that wealth received long-lasting well-being and greatness. The 27th verse. "Iravu Kanavil Ezhuntharuli Enbal Anbal Eppozhuthum Paravum Cheran Thanaku Unakku Paimpon Kanam Pattadai Viravu Kathirsey Navamani Poon Venditru Ellam Kuraivintri Thara Nam Olai Tharukindrom Thazhathu Egi Varuga Enru" is how this verse is structured. In the night, the Lord appears in Panabathirar's dream. He reveals Himself. He says, "We are giving a sacred letter to the Chera, who always praises Us with love. We are giving you a sacred letter so that he will give you all the green gold, coins, silk clothes, and ornaments with radiant gems, and all that you desire, without any lack. Go to the Chera without delay. Give the sacred letter We have given to the Chera." Thus, Lord Somasundara, the Lord of Thiru Alavai, informs him. The 28th verse. "Athirkazhal Uthiyar Ventarku Arul Seytha Perumaiyale Ethiril Selvatukku Ettra Irinithi Kodukka Enru Mathimali Purisai Ennum Vasagam Varintha Vaimai Kathiroli Virintha Thottu Thirumugam Koduthar Kana" is the verse. He graciously gave a sacred letter, written on a radiant leaf, with the truthful words beginning "Mathimali Purisai," so that the Chera king, who wears resounding anklets, would give immense wealth befitting his incomparable riches, due to the greatness of the grace bestowed upon him. It is a very important point to note that this sacred letter was given to Panabathirar, and its greatness shines through the fact that it was given to the Chera. Even though the Pandya and Chola kings were hereditary devotees, the fact that the Lord gave a sacred letter to the Chera king shows the greatness bestowed upon the Chera king here. Along with giving the sacred letter, the Lord's sacred words within it, such as "Paruvak Kon Muppadiyena," exemplify the generosity of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. We bow down to the fact that the Lord Himself, through this Thirumuga Pasuram, revealed the greatness of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman sings here, "Selvatukku Ettra Irinithi Kodukka," as if explaining the meaning of the Thirumuga Pasuram, "Manporul Koduthu." The idea is that charity should be appropriate not only to the worthiness of the recipients but also to the wealth of the givers. Thus, Somasundara Peruman graciously gives the sacred letter written on a palm leaf. This is a sacred letter written on a palm leaf. "Thirumugam" is a respectful term for letters written by elders. That Thirumuga Pasuram, graciously given by the Lord of Thiru Alavai, is compiled as the first prabandham in the 11th Thirumurai by Nambiyandar Nambigal Perumanar. This prabandham is composed in Nerisai Asiriyappa. The Thirumuga Pasuram is as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Mathimali Purisai Madak Koodal Pathimisai Nilavu Palnira Varichiragu Annam Payil Pozhil Alavayil Manniya Sivan Yan Mozhitharu Matram Paruvak Kon Muppadiyena Pavalarkku Urimaiyin Urimaiyin Uthavi Oli Thigazh Kuruma Mathipurai Kulaviya Kudaikkezh Seruma Ukakkum Cheralan Kanka Pan Pal Yazh Payil Panapathiran Thanpal Enpal Anban."

"Anbal Kanpathum Karuthi Ponthanan Manporul Koduthu Varavidupathuve" Thiruchitrambalam is the Thirumuga Pasuram graciously given by the Lord of Thiru Alavai. Its meaning is: "O Chera king, who drives a war elephant, seated under a white royal umbrella that shines like a full moon, who helps poets with all they need, like a seasonal cloud, with rights on both sides, know this message from Me, Shiva, who resides permanently in Thiru Alavai, surrounded by groves where swans with milk-white, striped wings, which shine on the city of Madurai with its mansions and moon-filled ramparts, dwell. Panabathiran, who practices the lyre with appropriate melody, is as loving to Me as you are. He has come with this sacred letter to see you. Send him back with valuable gifts." "Mathimali Purisai" refers to the great height of the ramparts. It can also refer to ramparts surrounding the city and ramparts surrounding mansions. We see that Thirugnanasambandar Peruman praised the ramparts surrounding the Thiru Alavai temple as "Kapali Neel Kadimathil Koodal Alavai." The rampart of the Thiru Alavai temple is indeed named "Kapali Mathil." The Lord begins with "Mathimali Purisai" by mentioning the moon, "Mathi." The tradition of the Pandyas is the lunar lineage, the Chandra Vamsam. "Mathi" also has the connotation of subtle intellect. The historical reference to the divine play of Nanmadak Koodal is where the Lord refers to Madurai as "Padam Madak Koodal." "Alavai" is the name of the temple. The historical reference to the divine play of Alavai is here. The Lord says, "Alavayil Manniya Sivan Yan Mozhitharu Matram," meaning, "I, Shiva, residing in Alavai, speak this message." This is the traditional opening for writing letters, for writing sacred letters. We still see this tradition of writing from so-and-so to so-and-so. Our Lord, who mentions to whom it was written later as "Cheralan Kanka," thus mentions from whom this sacred letter comes as "Alavayil Manniya Sivan Yan Mozhitharu Matram." "Paruvak Konmuppadiyena" means that the Chera king helps poets with all they need, like a water-laden cloud in the rainy season. "Urimaiyin Urimaiyin Uthavi" is repeated twice to emphasize the right on both the giver's and the receiver's side. The repetition in this verse also denotes abundance. "Kuruma Mathipurai Kudai" refers to the white royal umbrella, which is traditionally described as being like a full moon. The Lord here describes this white royal umbrella as a full moon, referring to its color, shape, and coolness. "Seruma Ukakkum" is mentioned. "Seruma Ukakkum Cheralan Kanka" means, "O Chera king who drives an elephant." As the saying goes, "Velamudaitthu Malainadu" (the mountain country possesses elephants), the Chera country was rich in elephants. We will later see instances where the Chera king honors Nambigal by having him ride on an elephant. "Seruma" here refers to the elephant army, which is the best among the four types of armies. We also see here a subtle hint that if "Ma" is interpreted as a horse, the Nayanar was skilled in riding horses, and he is about to travel to Thirukailai on a horse. Panabathirar, who was skilled in all aspects of music, is revealed by the Lord of Thiru Alavai Himself as being as loving to Him as the Chera king. Therefore, the Lord has written, "Panabathirar has brought this sacred letter to you with the intention of seeing you. Send him back with valuable gifts." "Manporul" (valuable gifts) is

wealth earned through righteousness. It is a pride for wealth earned through righteousness. Such wealth earned through righteousness should be given with love. This also emphasizes the greatness of the gift. Thus, Lord Somasundara of Thiru Alavai graciously gives this sacred letter to Panabathirar. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 09 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. The Periya Puranam, Part 945, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Lord Somasundara graciously bestows the sacred letter upon Panabathirar. The 29th verse. "Sangap Pulavar Thirumugam Than Thalaimezh Kondu Pathiranar Angu Appozhuthe Purappattu Malainadu Anaiya Vanthu Eythi Thungap Parisai Kodungolur Thannil Pugunthu Thunnu Kodimangul Thodakkum Maligai Mun Vanthu Mannarkku Arivithar." Panabathirar, carrying the sacred letter given by Lord Somasundara, the Sangam poet, on his head, immediately set out from there, reached the mountain country, entered Kodungolur with its mighty ramparts, and came before the king's palace to inform him. Lord Somasundara Himself was the head of the Sangam poets in the first Sangam. It is a historical fact that Lord Shiva of Thiru Alavai was known by the name Iraiyanar. We have seen this as "Thirunthiya Nool Sangathul Anru Irunthu Tamil Arayntha Aruliya Anganar." He was also a Sangam poet and gave the sacred letter by singing a pasuram, thus he was a poet. Panabathirar, carrying it on his head, immediately set out from there, reached the mountain country, entered Kodungolur with its mighty ramparts, and came before the king's palace. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman shows in this verse how quickly Panabathirar reached there, as Iraiyanar had told him to go without delay. The path from Madurai to the Chera country is mentioned in Silappathikaram as the path taken by Kannagi, which is plausible here. Panabathirar informs the gatekeepers about his arrival with the Lord's sacred letter, and the gatekeepers go and inform the king. The 30th verse. "Ketta Pozhuthe Kai Thalaimezh Kondu Kilarnda Peranbal Nattam Pozhi Neer Vazhinthu Ezhiye Ezhunthu Nadukkam Miga Eythi Ottath Thampon Maligaiyin Purathil Urugum Sinthaiyudan Pattin Thalamai Pananar Patham Palakal Panikindrar." As soon as he heard the news, he clasped his hands over his head, and with overflowing love, tears streamed from his eyes. He rose, trembling greatly, and came out of his golden palace. With a melting heart, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar bowed many times at the feet of Panabathirar, the chief of poets. The 31st verse. "Adiyen Porula Thirumugam Kondu Anaithathu Enna Avar Thammum Kodiser Vidayar Thirumugam Kaikkoduthu Vananga Kotravanar Mudimezh Kondu Koothadi Mozhiyum Kuralari Pozhi Kannir Podiyar Marbil Paranthu Vizha Puvimezh Palakal Vizhnthu Ezhunthar." Cheraman Perumal Nayanar said, "You have brought this sacred letter, considering me as someone worthy," and bowed to Panabathirar. We will later see him say, "I did not know the extent of the grace of the sacred letter that made me worthy." We have seen phrases like "Ennaiyum Porula Innarul Purintharulum" (He will graciously bless me as well) and "Ennai Oru Porula Konde Embiran Arul Seytharal" (If my Lord has blessed me, considering me as someone worthy). As Cheraman Perumal Nayanar bowed to Panabathirar, Pananar also gave him the sacred letter from the Lord and bowed. The king received the sacred letter, placed it on his head, danced with joy, his voice choked, and tears of joy streamed from his eyes, flowing over his chest adorned with sacred ash, and he fell to the ground many times and rose again, worshipping. The 32nd and 33rd verses. "Parivil Potri Thirumugathai Palakal Thozhuthu Padiyedukka Uriya Vagaiyil Eduthu Othi Umbar Peruman Arul Potri."

"Veri Ponchudar Maligai Pokku Mevum Urimai Sutramellam Perithu Viraiyil Koduponthu Penu Amaicharkku Arul Seyvar. Nangal Kula Maligai Ithanul Nalathin Mikka Nithikkuvaiyam Ponggi Niraintha Palveru Vagaiyil Polintha Pandaram Angan Onrum Ozhiyama Adaiyak Kandu Purappattu Thangum Pothisai Thalin Mel Samaiya Ettikonarum Ena" are these two verses. With love, he praised and bowed many times to the sacred letter. He carefully unrolled the sacred letter given by the Lord and read it. Praising the grace of the Lord, the God of gods, he entered his golden palace and summoned all his relatives and ministers. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar then instructed his ministers, "Bring all the treasures from the treasury within our royal palace, which is filled with abundant and various kinds of wealth, so that not a single item remains. Pack them properly and have them carried by men." The 34th verse. "Cheraman Peruman Arul Seyya Thirunthu Mathinool Manthirigal Sarum Manimaligai Ullal Thanangal Ellam Niraintha Perum Sirkol Nithiyum Enniraintha Ellam Pothisai Thal Alin Mel Paril Nerunga Misai Ettikondu Vanthu Panintharkal." As Cheraman Peruman Nayanar thus commanded, the wise and learned ministers brought all the immense and countless treasures, packed them into bundles, and carried them on men's shoulders, and presented them to the king, bowing. The 35th verse. "Parantha Nithiyin Parappu Ellam Pananar Pathiranarukku Nirantha Thanangal Veru Veru Niraithukkatti Matru Ivaiyum Uram Thangiya Vem Kari Parigal Muthalam Uyirullana Thanamum Puratha Arasum Kollum Ena Mozhinthar Poraiyar Puravalanar." The Chera king, the protector of the earth, showed Panabathirar all the vast and varied treasures, one by one. He said, "Take all these, and also accept the living treasures such as mighty elephants and horses, and also accept the kingdom that I rule." Thus, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar gave all these to Panabathirar. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar gave Panabathirar all the living treasures such as elephants, horses, and cows, and all other treasures, and also the right to rule the kingdom. The 36th verse. "Pananar Pathiranarum Paimpon Mauli Cheralanar Kana Kodutha Nithiyellam Kandu Magizhuvutru Athisayithu Pena Enakku Venduvana Adiyen Kolla Pinjaganar Anai Arasum Arasu Uruppum Kaikkondu Arulum Ena Irainja." Panabathirar, the poet, was delighted and amazed to see all the treasures shown by Cheraman Perumal, who wore a golden crown. Amazed, he said to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, "Let me take what I need. Please accept the kingdom and its components yourself. This is the Lord's command," and bowed. According to the dharma shastras, taking back what has been given is not permissible. However, Panabathirar gives back the kingdom given by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar himself. He says, "This is the Lord's command. Give me only what I need. You must keep the kingdom and its components. This is the Lord's command."

Cheraman Perumal Nayanar accepts it again. To avoid going against the Lord's command, he takes back the things he had given from Panabathirar. The 37th verse. "Iraivar Anai Maruppathanukku Anji Isainthar Igalventar Niraiyum Nithiyin Parappu Ellam Nilathai Neliya Udan Konde Urai Mummamathathu Kaliru Pari Ullittana Venduvana Kondu Or Pirai Venkottu Kaliru Mel Kondu Pontharum Perumpanar." The Chera king, fearing to disobey the Lord's command, accepted back the kingdom he had given to Panabathirar. Now, Panabathirar, taking with him all the abundant treasures, making the very earth bend, and taking only what he needed from the elephants and horses, set out towards Madurai on an elephant with crescent-shaped white tusks. The 38th verse. "Panbu Perugum Perumalum Pananar Pathiranar Pin Kankal Pozhintha Kathal Neer Vazhiya Kaiyal Thozhuthu Anaiya Nanbu Sirakkum Avar Thammai Nagarin Purathu Vidai Kondu Thin Porporisai Thiru Madurai Pukkar Thirunthum Isaippanar." Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, whose character was filled with devotion to Shiva, followed Panabathirar, his eyes shedding tears of joy, his hands clasped in reverence, until the city limits. It is customary to walk fourteen or seven paces behind elders when seeing them off. Here, due to his overflowing love, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar followed Panabathirar until the city limits. Even there, he stood there, overcome with emotion, unable to bid farewell to Panabathirar. Panabathirar himself left after bidding farewell. Panabathirar, having taken leave of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, entered the sacred city of Madurai, surrounded by strong golden ramparts. The 39th verse. "Vana Varambar Kulam Perukkum Mannanarum Marithu Egi Konal Ilam Ven Piraikanni Mudiyar Adimai Kondu Arulum Panmai Arulin Perumaiyinai Ninaithu Palakal Padinthu Ethi Menmai Vilangu Maligai Mandapathul Arasu Vitrirunthar." Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the king who enhanced the lineage of the Cheras, returned from the city limits to the city. He then sat on his throne in the royal assembly hall, reflecting repeatedly on the greatness of the Lord Shiva's grace, who wears the crescent moon on his matted hair, and how he graciously accepts his devotees. He was overcome with emotion, thinking about the message that Lord Somasundara had graciously given him for Panabathirar. The fact that Lord Somasundara gave a sacred letter to Panabathirar was not to bestow wealth upon Panabathirar. Nor was it to seek wealth for himself, because Lord Somasundara is the great Siddha Sri Vallabha Siddha, who Himself performed the divine plays of "Uzhava Keli" and "Uzhava Kottai" and owns the entire world. However, the compassion with which he did not directly give great wealth to Panabathirar but instead gave a sacred letter and had him receive it from Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, is the greatness of the compassion that made him accept himself through Panabathirar. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, reflecting on this with a melting heart, sat in the royal assembly hall and continued to attend to royal duties. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 10 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. The Periya Puranam, Part 946, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. The 40th verse. "Alavil Perumai Akila Yonikalum Kazharitru Arinthu Avatrin Ulam Manniya Meyyuru Thuyaram Onrum Ozhiya Vagai Agatri Kalavu Kolaikal Muthalana Kadinthu Kazharitrarivar Tham Valavar Perumanudan Sezhiyar Magizhum Kalappil Magizhum Nal." This verse describes Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, our Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, as one who rules by removing all the sorrows arising in the hearts of all living beings under his rule. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar is one who knows all that is spoken by living beings from all countless species and removes their sorrows. This was a boon he received from the Lord when he accepted the kingship. He prayed to the Lord for the ability to hear and understand all the languages spoken by all living beings. That is why our Nayanar was named Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, as we have seen. Therefore, he sought this boon to know and alleviate the sufferings of all living beings under his rule. Moreover, he not only protects the non-human beings but also ensures the well-being of the people under his rule. He protects the people by eliminating theft, murder, and other such evils. He rules in friendship with the other two Tamil kings, the Chola and Pandya monarchs, without enmity. The 41st verse. "Vankangai Nathi Pothintha Malku Sadaiyar Vazhipattu Thu Nar Sirappin Archanaiyam Thondu Purivar Thamakkoru Nal Then Nakkalarnda Kondraiyinar Adal Silambin Oli Munpol Mana Pussai Mudivinkan Kelathu Ozhia Mathi Mayangi" is the verse. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar is one who performs the sacred service of Shiva Puja, which is the pure and excellent worship of the Lord. We have already seen that in the Shiva Puja performed by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the Great Dancer would make the sound of His anklets audible to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. At such a time, one day, in the puja performed by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the sound of the Lord's dancing anklets was not heard at the end of the puja, as it usually was. The Lord making the sound of His anklets audible at the end of every puja is a sign that He accepts the puja and bestows His grace. But on this particular day, the sound of the Lord's anklets was not heard. Therefore, it is the nature of great souls to believe that if they do not receive the Lord's grace, the fault lies with them, and to try to rectify it. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar also thinks the same way. The 42nd verse. "Pusai Kadithu Mudithu Adiyen Enno Pizhaithathu Ena Porumi Asai Udambal Matru Ini Veru Adaiyum Inbam Yathu Enru Thesin Vilangum Udavalai Uruvi Thiru Marbinil Nata Isar Virainthu Thiruchilambin Osai Migavum Isaippithar." Realizing that the sound of the anklets was not heard, he sorrowfully completed the puja very quickly. The Lord would bestow the sound of the anklets at the end of the puja. The end of the puja mentioned here refers to the completion of the worship rituals for Lord Shiva. At the end of it, He would make the sound of the anklets. After the completion of the Lord's puja, according to the Sivagama rules, the remaining rituals, such as Chandeswara Puja and Puja Odukkam, are also parts of the puja. Therefore, he quickly completes these as well. He had made a mistake in the puja. That is why the Lord did not bestow the sound of His sacred anklets as a sign. He grieved and lamented, "What mistake have I made in the puja?"

He decided, "What joy can I attain with this body that I now bear? What is the use of this body?" Cheraman Perumal Nayanar thought, "This body that I carry with desire, if I cannot attain grace through the puja performed with this body, then why should I continue to live with this body? This body is for worshipping Lord Shiva and attaining the pure joy of His grace. If this body cannot perform the puja to Lord Shiva properly, then what is the purpose of this body?" As it is said, "Oonadaintha Udambin Piraviye Thanadaintha Uruthiyai Charumal." Therefore, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, determined that the only thing to do now was to destroy this useless body that could not perform a puja acceptable to the Lord, drew his sword and was about to plunge it into his sacred chest. At that very moment, the Lord quickly made the sound of His sacred anklets, even louder than usual. He made the sound even louder than He usually did. Hearing that, the Nayanar, who was in that state, heard the 43rd verse. "Adal Silambin Oli Kela Udaval Agatri Angai Malarkura Thalaimezh Kuvithu Arulikondu Vizhunthu Thozhuthu Ezhunthu Nidap Paravi Mozhikindrar. Nedumal Piraman Arumarai Mun Thedatku Ariyai Thiruvarul Mun Seyyathu Ozhinthathu En Enrar." Hearing the sound of the dancing anklets, he dropped the sword he was holding, clasped his beautiful lotus-like hands over his head, fell to the ground, worshipped, rose, and praised the Lord at length, saying, "O Lord, who is difficult for the great Vishnu, Brahma, and the rare Vedas to find! Why did you not bestow your grace before?" He asks, "Unless you reveal it, living beings cannot know. Therefore, you must reveal it." He asks the Lord the reason why the anklet sound was not heard before, because he wants to know his mistake and rectify it. The 44th verse. "Enra Pozhuthil Iraivar Tham Ethirnintru Arulathu Ezhum Oliyal Manrinidai Nam Koothadal Vanthu Vanangi Vanthondan Onrum Unarval Nammai Potri Uraisey Pathikam Paduthalal Nintru Ketu Varath Thazhthuvum Enrar Avarai Ninaippippar." When Cheraman Perumal Nayanar asked, the Lord, without appearing before him, spoke in an ethereal voice, "Our great devotee, Vanthondan, came and worshipped Our divine dance in the hall, and with a unified consciousness, praised Us and sang a sacred hymn. We delayed coming here because We stood and listened to it." Thus, the Lord reminds him of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. "Manru" refers to Thillai Thiru Chidambaram. Although "Chidambaram" is everywhere, "Manru" specifically refers to the Thillai Ambalam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, in his yogic state, always worshipped within his heart, without interruption, wherever he was. He visited many sacred places in this world and, upon reaching Thillai, he worshipped and sang a sacred hymn. The Lord refers to him as "Vanthondan" in this verse. His history of being forcibly taken and graced by the Lord is known to all of us. Such a Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sings a sacred hymn with a unified consciousness, with a single understanding, without separation, in a state of advaita. He sings in a state where he expresses the Shiva experience, which transcends words and understanding, through words. This sacred hymn sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, referred to by the Lord, was sung when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, after worshipping Thiru Perur, came to Thillai for the second time and sang the hymn beginning "Madithadum," which praises the state he experienced in Perur as having attained the Lord of Perur in the Chidambaram of Puliyur.

We reflected on that sacred history in the puranam of Eyerkon Kalikkama Nayanar. Therefore, when Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang that sacred hymn, the Lord stood within it, listened to it, and was established within its meaning. Therefore, the Lord said that He was delayed in coming here. The reason the Lord gave this reason was to remind Cheraman Perumal Nayanar about Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Although the Lord is omnipresent and capable of doing everything at once, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman beautifully shows us that this miraculous act of grace by the Lord, saying that He was delayed in coming here because He listened to the sacred hymn in Thillai, was to remind Cheraman Perumal Nayanar of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 45th verse. "Enne Adiyarkku Ivar Arulum Karunai Irunthavaru Enru Ponnar Sadaiyar Thirunadam Sey Puliyur Ponnamplam Irainji Thanner Illa Vanthondar Thammaiyum Kanpan Ena Virumbi Nannir Nattuch Chela Nayanthar Namachcherar Komanar" is this verse. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar marvels, "What a compassion this Lord has for His devotees!" He desires to go to Chidambaram, also known as Perumattrapuliyur, where the Lord, with golden matted hair, performs His sacred dance, and worship the Golden Hall. He also wishes to see Vanthondar, who is incomparable. Perumattrapuliyur is Chidambaram. The golden hall in Puliyur is Ponnamplam. The Lord said that He was delayed in coming here because He listened to Nambigal's singing while worshipping His divine dance there. Therefore, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar also desired to go and see that Ponnamplam. Although the Nayanar, from Kodungolur, did not go directly to Chidambaram, he heard the sound of the sacred anklets daily at the end of his worship in a yogic state. However, since the Lord Himself made the sound of the anklets audible daily, he now desired to see the Lord in person, in accordance with this great act of grace. Not only did he wish to see and worship the Golden Hall of the Lord's divine dance, but he also wished to see Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is praised in this verse as "Thanner Illa Vanthondar," meaning "incomparable Vanthondar." "Thanner Illa" means "without equal." Thirukkural says, "Thanukku Uvamai Illan" (He who has no equal). The Lord is incomparable, and so is Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, His friend whom He graced. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is incomparable. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is so great that the Lord Himself said that He was delayed in coming here because He listened to his sacred hymn. Therefore, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar desired to go to the Chola country, which is a land of good water, to see and worship him. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 11 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. The Periya Puranam, Part 947, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar resolves to travel to the Chola country, have a darshan of Chidambaram, and then meet Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 46th verse. "Ponnar Mauli Cheralanar Potrum Amaicharkku Agutha Iyambi Nannal Kondu Perum Payanam Ezhuga Enru Nalam Satra Minna Ayilvel Kula Maravar Venri Nilavum Silai Virar Annattullar Adaiya Nirantha Anainthar Vanji Agannagar Vay." Cheraman Peruman Nayanar expresses his desire to his esteemed ministers. His ministers are those who protect the royal affairs of Cheraman Peruman Nayanar without fail. "Potruthal" means to protect. So, they are protecting ministers. This refers to the protection of political principles. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, the king of the Chera country, is traveling to another great country, the Chola country, beyond his own. Therefore, the necessary arrangements must be made according to royal protocol. The ministers are responsible for carefully selecting and making the necessary arrangements for the king's journey. Moreover, until the king returns from his visit to another king's country, the ministers are responsible for all the necessary arrangements for the political administration of the country during that interim period. Therefore, with all this in mind, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, as king, informs his ministers about his journey. Thus, the king commands them to choose an auspicious day and prepare for the grand journey. It was a long-standing custom among the elders in our country that journeys should begin on auspicious days. That custom brought good results. However, in these days, people neglect it. Accordingly, we see that the benefits of such journeys also slip away. The journey mentioned here is a "perum payanam" (grand journey), which is a long and extended journey. Moreover, it is a journey that can yield great benefits through divine worship and guru worship. Therefore, it is a grand journey. As the king thus commanded, the archers, other spear-wielding warriors, and other people from that country gathered in the city of Vanji, Kodungolur. The 47th verse. "Itta Nannal Ooraiyinil Iraivar Thiru Anjaikkalathu Mattu Viri Poonkondraiyinar Thammai Valam Kondu Irainji Poy Patta Nuthal Venkali Yanai Pidarmezh Kondu Panimathiyum Thotta Kodi Maligai Muthur Kadanthaar Uthiyar Thonthalar." An auspicious day and an auspicious hour are chosen for the king's journey. First, the place from which to depart. Then, the day and time of departure. Those skilled in astrological texts calculate and tell these. Moreover, that auspicious day must also be a day pleasing to the Lord. The elders also command that good deeds should be done on such a day pleasing to the Lord. A day pleasing to the Lord is mentioned in Thevaram as "Athirai Nal Ukanthanum." Such an auspicious day. Along with that, an auspicious hour, that is, a good time, muhurtham, lagna, also determine that auspicious time. Our ancestors have classified certain stars for travel. They have also classified stars that are not suitable for travel. We see that such methods and customs of calculating and acting according to astrological texts are still prevalent among the people of the mountain country, the Chera country. On such an auspicious day, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar goes before the sacred shrine where Lord Thiru Anjaikkalathu Peruman resides and worships Him. The Sthala Vriksham (sacred tree) at Thiru Anjaikkalam is the Konrai tree. Therefore, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar goes before the Lord adorned with Konrai flowers.

Lord Anjaikkalathu Mahadevar is worshipped as the spiritual leader of the Chera kings. Therefore, it is a very good practice to worship one's Lord before undertaking such special tasks. Thus, the king sets out after worshipping. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar crosses the ancient city of Kodungolur, riding on the neck of an elephant with a decorated forehead. The 48th verse. "Yanai Anikal Paranthu Vazhi Engum Nirantha Selluvana. Mana Malai Nattinil Malintha Malaikaludan Povathu Ponra Senai Virar Pudai Paranthu Selvathu Angan Malai Suzhntha Kanam Adaiya Udan Padarvathu Polum Katchi Mevinathal." Elephant troops, adorned, spread out and move along the entire path. The army of warriors, accompanying the king, appears like the abundant mountains of the glorious mountain country moving along, and the surrounding forest accompanying the mountains. Thus, by using similes, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman shows us the glory of the king's journey and the might of his army. The elephant army and such warriors accompany the king to see him off. This mountain country was formerly a land where Parasurama, by his boon, caused the sea to recede for some distance, revealing land, which he then made into a country, and later, that mountain country became a land where fields were cultivated. Therefore, before the advent of agricultural fields, there were mountainous regions (Kurunji), forests (Mullai), and coastal areas (Neythal). In this verse, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman says that the elephants and warriors are like mountains and forests. In the next verse, he will describe the beauty of the coastal area, the Neythal land. The 49th verse. "Puravith Thiralkal Ayogap Polivin Asaivil Pothuvana Aravach Senai Kadal Tharangam Aduthu Mel Mel Adarvana Pol Viravip Paranthu Senranaval Misaiyum Avalum Onraga Niravip Parantha Nedunsenai Nemi Nezhiya Senranaval." The cavalry troops move with a graceful sway. The expanse of the army is like the sea. Just as waves in the sea rise high and spread out more and more as they reach the shore, the cavalry troops spread out and move forward with their heads held high. It is a mountainous country with ups and downs. But these armies move in such a way that the ups and downs become one. As a result, the earth itself bends and becomes pliable and yielding. The 50th verse. "Annattu Ellai Kadantha Anaiya Amaicharkku Ellam Vidai Aruli Minnar Manip Poon Mannavanar Venduvana Udan Kondu Konnar Ayil Vel Maravar Payil Kongar Nadu Kadantha Aruli Ponnattu Avarum Anainthu Adum Ponni Neer Nattidai Povar." The king is about to cross the border of his mountain country. Therefore, he bids farewell to all his ministers. The armies and ministers accompanied him to the border of his country to see him off. After crossing the border, when the king enters another country, it is not advisable for the armies and ministers to accompany him, nor is it necessary. Therefore, after crossing the border, the king bids farewell to his ministers and warriors and sends them back. He takes only those who are needed for his personal guard and service on the journey and sends the others back to their country. Thus, the king, with his retinue, crosses the border of his Chera country, enters the Kongu country, and then crosses it. The Kongars were a type of minor chieftain lineage. This country was named Kongu Nadu because it was ruled by them. Kongu Nadu is the region between the Chola kings in the north and the Chera kings in the south and west.

However, historical accounts show that these Kongu kings ruled independently without being subject to either of them. The fact that the four boundaries of this Kongu country were surrounded by hills favored their independent rule. Nevertheless, at times, they were subject to the rule of the Cheras and Cholas as minor chieftains, paying tribute. Moreover, this region has also been under the direct rule of the Cheras and Cholas at various times, as we learn from history. We see here that during the time of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the kings of this region ruled independently. After crossing such a Kongu country, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar reaches the Chola country, which is blessed with the waters of the Kaveri, where even celestial beings come and bathe. We see that there are many Shiva temples established and worshipped by many celestials who came and performed penance on the banks of the Kaveri. We have seen these in the histories of places like Mahendrapalli, Thiru Brahmapuram, Thiru Chakrappalli, Thiru Kannar Kovil, Thiru Purathi Niyamam, Thiru Nallaru, and so on. Such a country, where even celestial beings come and bathe in the Kaveri and perform penance, is the Ponni Neer Nadu, the Chola country. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar arrives in that Chola country. The 51st verse. "Senra Thisaiyil Sivanadiyar Sirappinodum Ethirkolla Kunrum Kadum Udai Kurumbar Idangal Thorum Kuraivara Upponrum Urambum Kanarum Thurukarsuramum Pala Kadantha Venri Vidayar Idam Palavum Mevip Paninthu Selkindrar." Wherever Cheraman Perumal Nayanar goes, Shiva devotees come to meet him. This is not because he is a king, but because he is a great devotee who is devoted to Lord Shiva and His devotees. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar sets out in that direction, desiring to see the Golden Hall of Puliyur and also Vanthondar. Therefore, he crosses all the regions in between. He crosses the regions of minor chieftains who rule small territories. He crosses the small countries where local leaders, who have hills and forests as their habitats, rule. Those minor kings respectfully provide and arrange everything needed for Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and treat him well. He crosses barren lands filled with pebbles, wild rivers, and stony paths. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar thus worships all the Shiva temples along his way. We have already seen the divine Sekkizhar Peruman describe the geographical features of these regions in detail in the Thirupuranam of Thirugnanasambandar Peruman, Thirunavukkarasar Peruman, and the Thirupuranam of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, which includes the puranam of Eyerkon Kalikkama Nayanar. We saw that Thirugnanasambandar Peruman came to this Kongu region from Thiruppainjili. We saw that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal crossed the Kaveri and came to the Kongu country from Thiru Ingoy Malai. Therefore, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar travels through these same regions. As he comes, he visits temples like Thirukkodumudi and Thirukkaruvur Anilai in the Kongu country. The 52nd verse. "Pozhivill Ponni Thirunathiyin Karai Vanthu Eythi Punitha Neer Maruvum Theertham Magizhthu Adi Marungu Vadapal Karaiyeri Thiruvill Pozhiyum Thiruppuliyur Sempon Manrul Nadam Potra Urugum Manathinudan Senrar Ozhiya Anbin Vazhi Vanthar" is the verse. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, who had crossed the Kongu country and reached the border region of the Chola country, arrived at the southern bank of the incomparable sacred Kaveri river. He then crossed to the northern bank of the Kaveri, joyfully bathed in its pure waters, and continued his sacred journey. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar travels along the southern bank of the Kaveri in the Chola country. We see that this Kaveri river is a sacred tirtha, a divine embodiment that purifies both externally and internally.

Thus, after bathing in the Kaveri and worshipping the temples, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar crosses to the northern bank of the Kaveri and proceeds to Chidambaram. Therefore, the path taken by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar is from Kodungolur, heading northeast through Pollachi, Dharapuram, Karur, reaching Tiruchirappalli on the southern bank of the Kaveri. From there, he crosses the Kaveri and proceeds along the road heading north and east through Lalgudi, Thirupazhur, and Gangaikonda Cholapuram, reaching Puliyur Thiruchitrambalam on the northern bank of the Kaveri. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, who came with unwavering love, having traversed such a long distance with continuous overflowing love, arrives at Chidambaram, also known as Perumattrapuliyur, which is the sacred Puliyur where the Lord, who daily performs the dance and makes the sound of the anklets audible, resides in the Golden Hall, and where the wealthy people of Thillai, the Brahmins who are the wealth of the world, live. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 12 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 948, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam, Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar reaches the boundary of the sacred city of Thillai. 53rd verse. When he arrived at the ancient city of Thillai, he bowed at its boundary with joy. As the groups of Brahmins and devotees approached, he bowed in return. He worshipped the fragrant, flower-filled sacred street, joining his hands above his head. With a joyful heart, Cheralanar reached the seven-tiered gopuram. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar reaches the sacred boundary of Thillai. Thillai has a special arrangement where its sacred boundary extends for a distance of a 'katham' (a measure of distance) in all four directions. As he reached this boundary, all the Brahmins of Thillai came forward to greet him with joy. Groups of devotees also arrived. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman states in this verse that the Brahmins of Thillai, due to their unique prominence, first came forward to greet him, and only then did the assembly of Shiva devotees come forward. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar bowed to the Brahmins of Thillai and the groups of devotees who had come to greet and worship him. Entering the sacred city of Thillai, he worshipped the beautiful, flower-adorned sacred street, joining his hands above his head with a joyful heart, and reached the seven-tiered gopuram. 54th verse. He bowed to the ground before the glorious seven-tiered gopuram, his eyes welling up with tears, and entered. He circumambulated the jeweled palace and worshipped the Thiru Perambalam, which illuminates the world. He then approached the Thiru Chitrambalam, where the Lord who created countless universes dances. The glorious seven-tiered gopuram. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar worshipped it according to tradition, entered through the sacred gopuram's entrance, and proceeded with due reverence. It is customary to prostrate on the ground before the sacred gopuram. Thus, prostrating on the ground, with tears welling up in his eyes, he entered and circumambulated the sacred palace. The sacred palace refers to the area within the sacred wall of the Lord's temple, known as the Thirumaligai Patti. After circumambulating it, he worshipped the Thiru Perambalam, which illuminates this world. Perambalam and Chitrambalam are different; they are two distinct halls. Perambalam is the hall where processional deities like Somaskandar always reside. Chitrambalam is the golden hall where Sabhanayagar, the Lord of Dance, resides.
