Thus, worshipping Kurumbala Nathar and Koothar Peruman in Thirukutralam, and visiting nearby places like Papanasam and Thiruppudaimarudur, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar arrive at Thirunelveli. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman's verses inform us that Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, who set out from Madurai, proceeded southwest along the road in the Western Ghats, via Srivilliputhur, Sivagiri, and Kadayanallur, directly to Thirukutralam, and then from there, along the southeast road via Thiruppudaimarudur, arrived at Thirunelveli. The Thiruppathigams composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal for these places he visited—Thirukutralam, Thirukurumbala, and Thirunelveli—are currently unavailable to us due to our lack of merit. The 108th verse. "Nelveli nitrazhagar thamaip paninthu padi nigazh palveru pathi piravum paninthu anbal vanthu anainthar val vedarai venri visayan ethir panri pinsel veda mudalvar amar thiru ramesvaraththu." Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar worship the Lord of Thirunelveli, known as Neetrazhagar (the beautiful one adorned with sacred ash). Neetrazhagar is one of the names of the Lord of Thirunelveli. After visiting and worshipping Nellaiyappar Swamigal, the Neetrazhagar of Thirunelveli, and singing a Thiruppathigam, they set out from there, visiting all the sacred sites on the way, and then, via coastal sites like Thiruchendil (Thiruchendur) and Thiru Uttarakosamangai, they arrive at Thirurameswaram. In this verse, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman praises the Lord of Thirurameswaram as "Vil Vedarai Venri Visayan Ethir Panri Pinsel Veda Muthalvar" (the primordial Veda, who, as a hunter, pursued the boar in front of the victorious Arjuna). The story found in the Mahabharata tells how the asura Mookasura, in the form of a boar, was hiding, sent by Duryodhana to kill Arjuna, who was performing penance to obtain the Pasupatastra. Lord Shiva, taking the form of a hunter, surrounded by His retinue of Nandi Ganas in the form of attendants and the Vedas in the form of dogs, came as a hunter and shot an arrow at Mookasura. Such a great Lord is the primordial Veda. He is enshrined in Thirurameswaram. Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar arrive at the sacred site of Thirurameswaram. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 20 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 956, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarga. The Puranam of Kazharitrarivar Nayanar. The 109th verse. "Mannum ramesvaraththu mamaniyai mun vanangi pannum thamizh thodai saaththi payilkinrar pambu anintha senniyar madoththaththu thirukkethichcharam sarntha sol malar malaigal saaththi thuraththe thozhuthu amarnthar." Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships the great gem-like Lord enshrined in the enduring Thirurameswaram, offering a garland of Tamil poems. However, the pathigam composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal is currently unavailable to us due to our lack of merit. Sundaramurthi Swamigal stays there for some days with Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, worshipping the Lord. At that time, from Rameswaram itself, Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships Thirukkethichcharam in Mathottam, located in Sri Lanka (Eelam), and composes a Thiruppathigam. Thirukkethichcharam is a sacred site in Sri Lanka (Eelam). There is a sea between Thirurameswaram and Thirukkethichcharam. Therefore, Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships from afar and composes this Thiruppathigam. We have already seen in the Puranams of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman and Sundaramurthi Swamigal that these two great saints composed pathigams for the sacred sites of North India while they were in Thirukalathi. Similarly, here, Sundaramurthi Swamigal worships Thirukkethichcharam from Thirurameswaram and composes a Thiruppathigam. Mathottam is the name of the village. Kethichcharam is the name of the temple. The pathigam composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal is the 80th Thiruppathigam in the Seventh Thirumurai, set in Nattapadai pann. The first and last verses of this pathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Naththar pudai gnanam pasu erin nanai kavizhvaai mattham matha yanai uri porththa mazhuvalan paththagiya thondar thozhu palavi en karaimel seththarelum panivan thirukkethichcharaththane." "Karaiyar kadal suzhntha kali madoththa nannagarul siraiyar pozhil vandi yazh seyyum kethichcharaththanai maraiyar pugazh uran adi thondan urai seytha kuraiya thamizh paththum sola kuda koduvinaiye." Thiruchitrambalam. We have looked at the Sthala Puranam of Thirukkethichcharam when contemplating the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. It is a place where sages like Bhrigu and the asura Maliyavan worshipped and attained blessings. Now, the 110th verse. "Thiru ramesvaraththu sezhum pavala sudar kozhunthai parivinal thozhuthu aganru paramar pathi pira paninthu peru vimanaththin imaiyor vanangu perum thiruchchuzhiyal maruvinar van thondar malai vendharudan kuda." Sundaramurthi Swamigal, with love, worships the Lord of Thirurameswaram, who is like a glowing coral sprout, and then departs, worshipping many other sacred sites of the Lord. These include Ilaiyankudi, Thanjakkai, and other such places. Thanjakkai is one of the Thevaram Vaipu Sthalams, also known as Thanjakkur. Thus, after visiting other sacred sites, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar arrive at the sacred site of Thiruchchuzhiyal. Thiruchchuzhiyal is a sacred site where celestial beings, traveling in their celestial vehicles, come and worship. The 111th verse. "Thiruchchuzhiyal idam konda sempon malai silaiyarai karuchchuzhiyil vizhamai kapparai kadal vidaththin irul suzhiyum midatrarai irainji ethir ithazhi malar paruchchuzhiyaththudan unaiyuyir enum pamalar punainthar." The Lord enshrined in Thiruchchuzhiyal is Thirumeninatha Swamy. He is the one who wielded the golden mountain Meru as a bow. He is the protector who prevents souls from falling into the whirlpool of rebirth (karuchchuzhi). He is the one whose throat is darkened by the poison that arose from the ocean. Falling before such a Lord, Sundaramurthi Swamigal offers a garland of words, beginning with "Unaiyuyir," along with a thick garland of Konrai flowers. "Paruchchuzhiyam" refers to a thick, coiled garland. "Suzhiyam" is a type of ornament worn by men. Thus, Sundaramurthi Swamigal composes a Thiruppathigam praising the Lord of Thiruchchuzhiyal. It is the 82nd Thiruppathigam in the Seventh Thirumurai, set in Nattapadai pann. There is also a tradition that the pann of this pathigam is Nattaragam. The Thiruppathigam composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal for the sacred site of Thiruchchuzhiyal is as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Unaiyuyir pugalaai agalidamaai mugil pozhiyum vanai varumathiyai vithi varuvanidam pozhilin thenathariththu isai vandinam mizhatrum thiruchchuzhiyal nanavidham ninaivar thamai naliyar naman thamare." "Nirur tharu nimalan thirumalaiyar kayal aruge therur tharum arakkan siram neriththan thiruchchuzhiyal perur ena urai van adi peyar navalar koman aruran thamizh malai paththu arivar thuyar ilare." Thiruchitrambalam. Thiruchchuzhiyal, also known as Thiruchchuzhi, is one of the 14 Thevaram-sung temples in the Pandya country. It can be reached from Madurai by going to Aruppukkottai and then a short distance east of Aruppukkottai. It can also be reached via Ramanathapuram and Paramakudi. The three temples of Thiruchchuzhi, Madurai, and Rameswaram have similar architectural structures. The Lord's names are Thirumeninathar, Suzagesar, Pralaya Vidangar, Thanunathar, Manakkolanathar, Kalyana Sundarar, Bhoomeeswarar, Bhoominathar. The Goddess's names are Sahayavalli, Thunaimalai Ammai, Swarnamalai, Muthumalai Umayal, Manikkamalai. The sacred waters are Pavagiri Nathi, Kavvai Kadal. This is the temple tank located opposite the temple. Bhoomi Theertham and Soola Theertham. Kaundinya Nathi serves as a sacred river. Lord Shiva once caused a deluge to swirl and enter the earth, hence the name Thiruchchuzhiyal. The Moolavar (presiding deity) is a Swayambhu Lingam. The Goddess is in a Tribhangi Lakshana posture, with her waist, neck, and left leg slightly bent in a dancing pose. This temple has 14 names: Vayalur, Mukthi Managar, Mukthipuram, Mukthinagar, Avarthapuram, Soolapuram, Soolanagar, Thrisoolapuram, Arasavanam, Bodhivanam, Suzhigai, Suzihapuri, Suzhiyal, Thiruchchuzhiyal. In Treta Yuga, Bhoomidevi worshipped Lord Shiva here. The Lila of the swirling deluge also occurred. The Lord showed the divine dance and wedding posture to Gautama and Ahalya here. The Sthala Puranam of this temple is found in the Kshetra Kanda of the Mahaskanda Purana, one of the 18 Puranas. The Saivite scholar Aara Amudhachariyar translated it into Tamil and composed it as the Sthala Puranam. On the day of Thirukkalyanam in the month of Panguni, the idols of Gautama and Ahalya are placed in front of the Lord, and the Lord and Goddess display their wedding posture as part of the traditional festival. Devas, Thirumal, Brahma, Indra, Manmathan, Surya, Chitradhwajan (the Kinnara chief), Chithraroopan, Nandeeswarar, Bhoomidevi—countless are those who worshipped the Lord here and attained blessings. Around the Thiruchchuzhiyal temple, eight Linga temples are situated. Many monasteries, choultries, Nandavanams (flower gardens), and mandapams are also present. Festivals are held every month of the year. Chithirai Vishu includes the reading of the Panchangam in the Kambathadi Mandapam. At night, the Lord and Goddess are adorned with Rakshabandhanam and go in a palanquin procession. Thiru Adippooram involves the Goddess being adorned with Rakshabandhanam, swinging on a swing, and then going in a car procession. Vinayagar Sashti, Avani Moolam Puttu festival. During Navaratri, the Goddess is seated in various forms for nine days. On Vijayadasami evening, Subramaniar, on a horse vehicle, goes to Pavagari (Kaundinya Nathi) for the arrow-shooting Lila. Aippasi Vishu, Kanda Sashti, Karthigai Somavaram, Thirukkarthigai, Dhanur Matha Puja in Margazhi, Arudra Darisanam, Uttarayana Punyakala Makara Sankranti, Thaipusam,

Pusam Masi Magam, and many other festivals. These are the special notes about the Thiruchchuzhiyal Sthalam. The 112th verse. "Anganaraip paninthuraiyum arurarkku avvuril kangulidai kanavin kan kalaiyam thiruvuruval sengkaiyil pon chendum thirumudiyil suzhiyamudan engum ila thiruvedam enburuga mun katti." To Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who worshipped and stayed with the Lord in Thiruchchuzhiyal, the Lord appeared in a dream at night in that village. He appeared in the form of a young bull (Kalai), holding a golden scepter in His sacred red hand and with a coiled ornament (suzhiyam) on His sacred head. This divine form, unseen anywhere else, was shown to Sundaramurthi Swamigal, melting his very bones. The Lord thus showed His form as a young bull. The age of Kalai (young bull) refers to the age of men who have completed 16 years. With such a Kalai form, the Lord appears with a golden scepter in His hand and a coiled ornament on His sacred head. He bestows this divine vision upon Sundaramurthi Swamigal at dawn. Along with bestowing this divine vision, the Lord also grants Sundaramurthi Swamigal a boon, saying, "We reside in Kaanapper," in that dream. The 113th and 114th verses. "Kaanapper yam iruppathu ena kazhari gangai enum vanapperarum ulavum mamudiyar tham agala gnanapperaralar unarnthu athisayiththu nagamudan enappereriyiru aninthar arul iruntha parisu enbar kandarumbadi kazharitrarivar kulamozhintharuli pundarika punal suzhiyal punithar kazhal vanangip poi andar piran thirukkanapper anivar arurar thondaradi thozhilum enum sorpathigath thodai punaiyar." The Lord declares, "We reside in Kaanapper." Kaanapper refers to the sacred site of Kalaiyar Koyil. The Lord, whose great matted locks are a place for the great river Ganga of the sky to flow, thus showed His divine form in a dream to Sundaramurthi Swamigal and also uttered, "We reside in Kaanapper," which is Kalaiyar Koyil. Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who possessed great wisdom, awoke from his sleep, astonished, and melted, saying, "What indeed is the nature of the Lord's grace that was upon me!" He then narrates the vision he saw and the words the Lord uttered to Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar. Then, worshipping the Lord of Thiruchchuzhiyal, Thirumeninatha Swamy, who resides amidst water bodies with lotus flowers, and taking leave, he sets out towards the Lord's sacred site of Thirukkaanapper and reaches near that sacred place. Sundaramurthi Swamigal then offers a garland of Tamil poems that begins with "Thondaradi Thozhilum." Sundaramurthi Swamigal approaches Kaanapper, also known as Kalaiyar Koyil, singing, "When will I be able to see and worship the Lord as Kalaiyar?" Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 21 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 957, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarga. The Puranam of Kazharitrarivar Nayanar. In Thiruchchuzhiyal, the Lord gave a divine vision and invited Sundaramurthi Swamigal to Kalaiyar Koyil. Sundaramurthi Swamigal, thinking, "When will I be able to see and worship the Lord as Kalaiyar?", approaches the Supreme Lord, praying to worship His feet daily. He reaches Kalaiyar Koyil. The blessing of daily worshipping His feet can only be bestowed by Lord Shiva Himself, and it cannot be attained without His grace. "Avan arulale avan thal vanangi" is from Thiruvasagam. "Thannai ninaiya tharuginran" is from the 11th Thirumurai, Vinayagar Irattaimanimalai, composed by Nambiyandar Nambigal. "Nalum parava thal tharuvar" in this verse means "to worship daily," that is, to perform Shiva Puja according to Shiva Agamic rules every day and praise Him. We have seen in the Thaduththatkonda Puranam how Sundaramurthi Swamigal sought and received the path of penance in Thiruththuraiyur and performed puja. We have also seen how the Lord commanded him, "Archanai patte agum athalal manmel nammai sotramizh paduga," and bestowed upon him the gift of singing Tamil poems. Accordingly, he approaches the Supreme Lord who grants the blessing of daily worshipping His feet. The 115th verse expresses this prayer. The 116th verse narrates the history of his reaching Thirukkalaiyar Koyil and composing a Thiruppathigam after such a prayer. The 115th and 116th verses are as follows: "Kalaiyar thamai kandu thozhappetruvadhu endru endru thalai nalum parava tharvarppal sargindrar. Alai nil idai kana anjiya nirnai ayale vazhai pay nuzhai pazhana munaippadi valanadar. Manna thirukkanapper valampathiyil vanthu eythi senni valar mathi aninthar sezhungkoyil valam kondu mun irainji ul anainthu mudalvar sevadi thazhnthu panna sezhunthamizh malai padinar paravinar." Sundaramurthi Swamigal arrives at the prosperous sacred site of Thirukkaanapper, also known as Kalaiyar Koyil. He circumambulates the rich temple of the Lord who wears the crescent moon on His head. He worships in front of it, enters, prostrates at the Lord's sacred feet, and sings and praises with a garland of rich Tamil poems. Kalaiyar is the name of the Lord of Thirukkaanapper. This Thiruppathigam praises the Lord, referring to the form of Kalai (young bull) that Sundaramurthi Swamigal saw in his dream, and it is composed as the refrain of the pathigam. This Thiruppathigam for the sacred site of Thirukkaanapper is the 84th Thiruppathigam in the Seventh Thirumurai, set in Puranirmai pann. The first and last verses of this pathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Thondar adi thozhilum sothi ilam piraiyum suthan menmulaiyal pagamum agi varum pundarika parisam meniyum vanavargal pusalida kadal nanjunda karuththamarum kondalena thigazhum kandamum en tholum kola narunjadai mel vannamum kankulira kandu thozhappetruvadhu endru kolo adiyen kar vayal suzh kanapper urai kalaiyaye." "Kannalai in amudhai kar vayal suzh kanapper urai kalaiyaye on sir urai thandhamizhal unni manaththu ayara ul urugi paravum on pozhil navalur kon agiya aruran pannum isaikkilavi paththu ivai padavallar paththar gunaththinarai eththisaiyum pugazh manni iruppavargal vanin ezhinidinum mandalanayakaraai vazhvathu nichchayame." Thiruchitrambalam. Sundaramurthi Swamigal proceeds towards Kalaiyar Koyil while singing this Thiruppathigam. Therefore, this was sung on the way, contemplating the sacred site of Kalaiyar Koyil and the Lord. The Thiruppathigam composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal after entering the temple is not available to us due to our lack of merit. The pann of this Thiruppathigam is Puranirmai, which is sung at dawn. Therefore, it is believed that Sundaramurthi Swamigal composed this at dawn when he arrived at this sacred place. Thirukkaanapper is the tenth of the 14 Thevaram-sung temples in the Pandya country. We have contemplated the special features of this temple in the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. The story of Airavatam worshipping Lord Shiva in this temple was mentioned in Thirugnanasambandha Peruman's pathigam. Accordingly, there is a sacred tank called Yanaimadu in front of the temple. Even today, we can see the temple elephant bathing there and worshipping the Lord. The 117th verse. "Aratha kathaludan appathiyil paninthu eththa sirarum thiruththondar silanal angu amarnthu aruli karararum malarcholai kanapper kadanthu anainthar por anetrar kayilai poruppar thiruppunavayil." With boundless love that was never satisfied by worship, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, the distinguished devotees, worshipped and praised in the sacred site of Kalaiyar Koyil. After staying there for some days, they passed through Thirukkaanapper, surrounded by flower gardens where clouds rested, and reached Thiruppunavayil of the Lord, who rides the mighty bull and is the Lord of Kailasa. The 118th verse. "Punavayil pathiyamarntha punithar alayam pukku manam arvura siththa ni ninai ennodu enre vinavana thamizh padi vizhnthu irainji appathiyil sinayanai uriththu aninthar thirupatham thozhuthu irundhar." Entering the temple of the holy Lord who lovingly resides in Thiruppunavayil, Sundaramurthi Swamigal, with great desire in his heart, sings a beautiful Tamil Thiruppathigam that seems to ask a question, beginning with "Siththa ni ninai ennodu." This Thiruppathigam composed by Sundaramurthi Swamigal for the sacred site of Thiruppunavayil is set in Pazhampanjuram pann. It is the 50th Thiruppathigam in the Seventh Thirumurai. The first and last verses of this pathigam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Siththam ni ninai ennodu suzharum vaigalum mattha yanaiyin iruri porththa manalan ur paththar tham palar padi ninru adum pazhampathi poththil anthigal pattara punavayile." "Podiyadum meniyan pon punan suzh punavayilai adiyar adiyan navalur an uraiththana madiyathu katru ivai eththa vallar vinai mainthu poi kudiyaga padi ninru ada vallarkku illai kutrame." Thiruchitrambalam. "My mind, which wanders with me, remember Thiruppunavayil every day" is the central theme of this Thiruppathigam, and we see the word "ninai" (remember) in every verse. The sacred site of Thiruppunavayil is the seventh of the Thevaram-sung temples in the Pandya country. We have contemplated its history in the Puranam of Thirugnanasambandha Peruman. The 119th verse. "Thiruppunavayil pathiyil amarntha sivanar magizhum viruppudaiya koyil pala paninthu arulal mevinar poruppinodu kan aganru punal ponni nadu anainthu paruppathavar silaiyar tham pambu ani managar thannil" is the verse. Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar worship many temples that Lord Shiva, who lovingly resides in Thiruppunavayil, delights in. In the Thiruppunavayil temple, temples of the other 13 Thevaram-sung temples in the Pandya country are also established. So, "many temples in the city" also refers to this. Now, from Thiruppunavayil, taking leave from the Lord, Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar visit the nearby sacred sites. Such nearby sacred sites, on the way to the Ponni country (Chola country), include Oriyur Vaipu Sthalam, Thirumanamelkudi, Thirupperunthurai (Avudaiyar Koyil), and other such sacred sites. The region from Thiruppunavayil to the border of the Chola country consists of small hills and small forests. These are the characteristics of the Pandya country up to Thiruppambani Nagar. Even today, we can see them in the same way. Thus, crossing hills and forests, they reach the water-rich Kaveri country, and Sundaramurthi Swamigal arrives at the sacred site known as Thiruppambani. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar also

arrives. The 120th verse. "Padhalichcharam irainji athan marungu palapathiyum vedathi nathar kazhal vanangi migu viraiyudan sutharum thunaimulaiyar manivaykku thotru iravu sethambal vaythirakkum thiruvarur vanthu anainthar." They go and worship at Thirupathalichcharam temple. The Sthala Puranam of Thirupathalichcharam states that Adiseshan emerged from a chasm and worshipped Lord Shiva here. "Pathal" refers to a chasm from which one comes from the netherworld to the earth. In such a sacred place, after worshipping the Lord, Sundaramurthi Swamigal quickly arrives at Thiruvarur. Many days had passed since he had been separated from the Lord of Thiruvarur due to his long pilgrimage. With great eagerness due to that longing, Sundaramurthi Swamigal quickly arrives at Thiruvarur. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar also accompanies him. Sundaramurthi Swamigal, who set out from Thirupathalichcharam, visits all the nearby sacred sites. These include Thiruppuvanur, Thiruidaivai, Thirupperaiyal, Thirunattiyaththan Kudi, Thiruvilamar, and other such places. After visiting all these, he arrives at Thiruvarur. These two great Nayanmars quickly reach Thiruvarur, worshipping the feet of the Lord, who is the primordial one of all Vedas. The 121st verse. "Thirunavalur vendhar serar kulavendhar udan varuvarai thiruvarur vazhvarhal ethirkolla tharum kathaludan vanangi tham peruman koyilin ul perugu arvaththodu virumbum peruperu perap pugunthar." The 122nd verse is a continuation. "Vasamalar konraiyar magizh koyil valam kondu nesamur mun irainji nedum pozhuthellam paravi esaraval thiruppathigam eduththu eththi ezhuntharulal pasavinai thodakkaruppar payil koyil paninthu anainthar." Nambi Sundaramurthi Swamigal, the king of Thirunavalur, arrives with Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, the king of the Chera lineage. All the residents of Thiruvarur come to receive them. They worship with great affection. Thus, worshipping both devotees, Sundaramurthi Swamigal enters the temple of his Lord with overflowing eagerness to receive the great blessing of worshipping Him with desire. We see here the tradition that whenever Sundaramurthi Swamigal returns to Thiruvarur, he first worships inside the temple before going to his divine mansion. He circumambulates the temple where the Lord, adorned with fragrant Konrai flowers, delights. He worships in front of Him with love, praises Him for long periods of worship, and then, due to the distress of long separation, which is called "esaravu," Sundaramurthi Swamigal sings and praises with a Thiruppathigam. After worshipping the Lord in this manner and taking leave from Him, they come out and are about to proceed to Paravai Ammaiyar's divine mansion, worshipping the temple where the Lord, who severs the bonds of karma, resides. Sundaramurthi Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 22 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 958, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar worship the Lord at the Thiruvarur temple. After that, they proceed to Paravaiyammaiyar's mansion. The 123rd verse. The attendants arrived first at Paravaiyammaiyar's mansion, where abundant and excellent decorations were made. The Lord of the mountain country, accompanied by the devotees, entered the golden mansion, where they were received with reverence. All the attendants first reached Paravaiyammaiyar's mansion. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar went directly to the temple upon arriving in Thiruvarur, the attendants had already reached the mansion and informed Paravaiyammaiyar of the arrival of both Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. Paravaiyammaiyar made extensive and auspicious decorations to welcome their arrival. She arranged for the decoration of the entrance, toranams (festive arches), flower garlands, full pots, rows of lamps, and other auspicious items. The term "vizhumai" refers to decorations made for the sake of devotees, while "per alankaram" refers to decorations made for the sake of a king. While Paravaiyammaiyar waited, having made all these decorations to receive them, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, accompanied by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the king of the mountain country, entered the beautiful mansion. Paravaiyammaiyar welcomed them with great love, humility, and praise. The 124th verse. Paravaiyammaiyar, with great affection, bowed and praised them. She prepared various kinds of food and curries, arranged the plates, floor coverings, and lamps, and served them a divine meal, she with her refined, honey-like words. As soon as Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar entered the mansion, Paravaiyammaiyar bowed and praised them, seated them on thrones, and performed all the necessary rituals as they should be done. Paravaiyammaiyar had prepared various kinds of divine food and curries befitting the guests. She had prepared various types of mixed rice dishes with ghee, sugar, green gram, and pepper, as well as other divine foods, rice, and many curries. After seating these two great personages and performing all the worship, she served them a divine meal. For this, Paravaiyammaiyar arranged the plates, the floor coverings, and the lamps in order, and served a divine meal to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, and all the other attendants. She was a woman of refined, honey-like words. Paravaiyammaiyar, by serving and feeding the devotees, had refined her speech. It is customary to speak sweet words and serve sweet food. Accordingly, she is praised as having honey-like words. We have contemplated the divine words from Periyapuranam, "after speaking endless sweet words with affection." Similarly, she served a divine meal with sweet words. In the puranams of Ilaiyankudi Mara Nayanar and Siruthonda Nayanar, and earlier in this puranam, if we gather all the explanations about Maheswara Puja, we can understand how devotees should be fed and how a divine meal should be served. The 125th verse. Delighted by the auspicious pujas performed by Paravaiyammaiyar, they stayed there happily. During all the abundant periods, they entered the Lord's temple, worshipped, and then came out, desiring good sports, never forgetting the grace of our Lord. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar stayed there happily, accepting the auspicious Maheswara Pujas performed by Paravaiyammaiyar. These Maheswara Pujas, performed for devotees, are the means to attain the eternal auspicious state of Shiva. During their stay, they entered and worshipped in the Lord's temple, the abode of the Lord who resides in the anthill, during all the special puja times, and then came out, desiring good sports, never forgetting the grace of our Lord. What kind of sports they witnessed is described by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman in the next verse, the 126th verse. They greatly rejoiced by playing the games of "nilai chendu" and "pari chendu." They witnessed the leaping of war elephants that are difficult to describe, and delighted in the elephant fights with long, thorny legs, which brought victory. They also enjoyed the bird fights of various kinds. Both great Nayanmars greatly rejoiced by playing the games of "nilai chendu" and "pari chendu." Playing "chendu" is a type of game similar to playing with a ball in these days. "Chendu" is a ball-like plaything. "Nilai chendu" means throwing it while standing. "Pari chendu" means playing by throwing the "chendu" while riding a horse, turning to the left and right. These are all games for men. Games like "kazhal," "pandu," "ammanai," "thol nokkam," and "oosal" are for women. In those days, there were separate games for men and women. These games were designed to suit the physical structure of men and women, and were natural games established by our ancestors in those days. But in these days, we see men playing women's games and women playing men's games. This is a perversion of time. Now, the games mentioned in this verse, such as "thagar paichal" (ram fighting), "varana por" (elephant fighting), and "pullin amar" (bird fighting), were all pastimes for people in our country. These were once enjoyed by the upper classes for entertainment. But in these days, we see that these types of games have been turned into gambling. These games have been degraded. Cockfighting and similar games. "Mutkanai kal varanam" refers to cockfighting. "Varanam" means a rooster. It is a type of rooster with long, sharp, thorn-like spurs on its legs. "Marappul" refers to birds like quails, partridges, and junglefowl. Both great Nayanmars also enjoyed watching these bird fights. However, while they witnessed these and similar things, other people in the world also witness them. But what the people of the world witness binds them to worldly attachments and subjects them to rebirths. Even though our great Nayanmars witnessed these very games, they did so without forgetting the grace of Lord Shiva. Therefore, these things did not bind them. The state of not forgetting grace was their salvation. That is why the divine Sekkizhar Peruman describes the games witnessed by these two great personages as "good sports." The 127th verse. As many days passed with overflowing love, the generous Chera king, whose mountain country is surrounded by the sea, bowed to Paravaiyammaiyar's husband and requested him to come. He agreed to come, having been worshipped day and night. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar stayed in Thiruvarur for a long time. At that time, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar requested Sundaramoorthy Swamigal day and night, bowing to him, to come to his mountain country, which is surrounded by the sea, and to take him along. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar as "Keralanar" in this verse. Kerala is the name of the Chera country. The custom is that what is called "Chera Nadu" or "Cheram" in Tamil is called "Kerala" in Sanskrit. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was one who did not consider the worldly pleasures of kingship as important, as he believed in "inbam isaintha inbam" (joy mixed with joy) and "inbathodu isaintha vazhvu" (life mixed with joy). Therefore, he did not readily agree to go to the mountain country with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. So, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar continuously requested Sundaramoorthy Swamigal day and night. Just as the Lord's nature is such that if one calls him for many days, he will think, "This one will not stop calling me for many days," as stated in the Thevaram, so too is the nature of his friend, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Therefore, as Cheraman Perumal Nayanar continuously requested, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal agreed to go to the Chera country. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 23 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 959, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal agrees to Cheraman Perumal Nayanar's request to visit the mountain country. He also seeks and obtains Paravaiyammaiyar's consent. The 128th verse. With the consent of Lady Paravaiyammaiyar, Nambi agreed to go. Cheraman Perumal, praising the grace of the Lord with the moon-crowned head, performed fitting pujas for all the devotees everywhere. With surging effort, both entered the holy temple of the Lord. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal agrees to go to the mountain country after obtaining the heartfelt consent of Lady Paravaiyammaiyar. Lady Paravaiyammaiyar is a heroine who cannot bear separation, so her consent was sought for the sake of enduring separation. Here, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's journey is not just a pilgrimage to a holy place, but a separation for the sake of a friend, so Paravaiyammaiyar's consent is sought here. We recall here that this consent was not mentioned during Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's previous pilgrimages. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar praises the divine grace that made Sundaramoorthy Swamigal agree to this, and that made both Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Paravaiyammaiyar give their consent. This is because such a great blessing is difficult to obtain without the Lord's grace. Therefore, with great joy, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar celebrated by offering Maheswara Puja, food, and other things. He considered Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's visit to the mountain country a rare and great blessing. At this point, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar enter the Thiruvarur Poonkoil (flower temple) and worship the Lord. The 129th verse. Having worshipped the Lord and received His grace, the devotees gathered around. Nambi Arurar and Kazharitrarivar, the king of the good Chera country with a long crown of pure gold and jewels, set out on their journey, worshipping Thiruvarur with its high golden walls, and proceeded westwards. After worshipping their Lord inside the Thiruvarur Poonkoil and receiving His gracious permission, these two great Nayanmars came out, and all the devotees joined them. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, the king of the good Chera country, crowned with pure gold, set out on their journey, worshipping Thiruvarur, and departed from there. They departed from Thiruvarur heading west. The path taken by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar towards the mountain country was northwest from Thiruvarur, along the southern bank of the Cauvery, visiting various holy places. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman describes this pilgrimage in the next verse, the 130th verse. They went along the southern bank of the Cauvery, which spreads gold and scatters gems and water, and worshipped many temples where Shiva rejoices. With minds melting in devotion, they bowed to Thirukkandiyur, where the Lord with the lightning-like matted hair resides, and then departed. The Cauvery is a sacred river that spreads gold, scatters gems, and flows with water. These two great personages travel along the southern bank of the sacred Cauvery. On their journey along the southern bank of the Cauvery, they visit and worship many temples where Lord Shiva rejoices. These temples are located northwest of Thiruvarur, on the southern bank of the Cauvery, such as Thirupperuvelur, Thirukkudavayil, Thiruppazhaiyarai, and Thirunallur. Thus, after visiting all these holy places, they reach Thirukkandiyur, where the Lord resides with pleasure. There, with minds melting in devotion, they go and worship the Lord of Thirukkandiyur.

He is Veerattaneswarar. Thirukkandiyur is the holy place where one of the eight heroic deeds (Ashta Veerach Cheyalkal) of the Lord, the plucking of Brahma's head, took place. There, with minds melting in devotion, they bow to the Lord and come out of the temple. The 131st verse. As Thiruvaiyaru appeared on the northern bank, their flower-like hands, with bodies and minds melting, were raised above their heads in reverence. They crossed the Cauvery, which flowed like a vast sea, and desired to worship the Lord's feet, which are ever connected. As soon as they came out of the Thirukkandiyur temple, the Thiruvaiyaru temple appeared opposite on the northern bank of the Cauvery. As they stood on the southern bank of the Cauvery from Thirukkandiyur, Thiruvaiyaru appeared on the northern bank, to the right of the Cauvery. As soon as the Thiruvaiyaru temple appeared, with their bodies and minds melting, they raised their flower-like hands above their heads in reverence. There, the Cauvery flowed like a vast sea. The Cauvery river flowed as if the sea itself had surged. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal desired to cross this Cauvery river, go to the northern bank, and worship the Lord's feet, which are ever connected. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman shows in the next verse that the Cauvery was then in great flood. The holy Cauvery appeared as if the sea had surged and spread for a long distance along the river. "The sea rose, its waves surged, and the Cauvery came with it. The land rose, and the conches and white pearls were scattered in Thiruvaiyaru," are the divine words of Thirugnanasambandar Peruman. This should be remembered here. The Lord residing on the northern bank of this Cauvery, after crossing the Cauvery, is the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru. His feet are the connected feet that embrace. The divine grace that continuously holds and saves souls. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal desires to worship that. Along with him, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar also desires to go to Thiruvaiyaru and worship. The 132nd verse. Seeing Thiruvaiyaru and worshipping it, Cheraman Perumal, from whom Lakshmi never departs, spoke to Arurar, who was full of grace. "My heart melts with desire to worship Thiruvaiyaru, where the Lord with the dark throat resides. Let us cross this river and worship Him." Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, from whom Lakshmi never departs. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman praises him as Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, from whom Lakshmi never departs, following the tradition that kings who rule the earth are aspects of Vishnu. This is seen in the divine words of Thiruvaimozhi, "If I see a king with a crown, I see Thirumal." Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, such a Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, seeing Thiruvaiyaru and worshipping it, spoke to Nambiyarurar, who was full of grace. "My heart melts with desire to go and worship this Thiruvaiyaru, where the Lord with the poison-filled throat resides. Let us cross this river and worship Him." He requests, "Let us worship the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru." Here, we, standing on the southern bank of the Cauvery, saw Thiruvaiyaru appearing on the northern bank and worshipped it with our minds. In addition, let us cross this Cauvery river and worship the Thiruvaiyaru temple with our bodies as well. This is the inner meaning. Thus, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar requests. But the Cauvery river there is flowing with great force, touching both banks, rising as if the sea is rising. A great flood is flowing as if touching the sky. The 133rd verse. The river, swelling and touching both banks, rose as if to the sky. As the water surged, preventing boats and ships from crossing, he bowed to the feet of the Lord whose sacred body is adorned with holy ash. After that, he sang, unable to bear the obstruction of the river. The Cauvery river, swelling and hitting both banks, flowed as if rising to the sky. The surging water, with its force, was eroding the banks and flowing. A great flood flowed as if reaching the sky. The water level of this river was so high and fast that even boats or large vessels could not cross it. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed to the feet of the Lord whose sacred body is adorned with holy ash. He is Nambigal Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who has the right to go through the river of love. This physical river obstructs him. But Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's river is the river of love for Shiva. Nothing can obstruct that river of love. Seeing this river obstructing him from worshipping the Lord, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, unable to bear it, called out to the Lord and sang. He called out to the Lord whose sacred body is adorned with holy ash and sang. The holy ash smeared by the Lord is a symbol of destruction and joy. The divine Sekkizhar Peruman mentions "Neeru vilangum thirumeni niruthar" here to indicate that worshipping Shiva will remove obstacles and bring joy. "Niruthar" refers to the dance of the five divine functions. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal called out to the Lord and sang. Let us continue to contemplate that divine history. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 24 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 960, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, standing on the southern bank of the Cauvery river, sings a Thiruppathikam (hymn) to the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru. The 134th verse. This verse describes how Vanthondar, with overflowing desire, stood and worshipped, singing the glorious Thiruppathikam, which he began with "Paravum Parisu" and ended each verse with "Aiyaru Udaiya Adigalo," calling out to the Lord who wears the serpent. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal begins the Thiruppathikam with "Paravum Parisu." In each verse of this Thiruppathikam, he ends with the refrain "Aiyaru Udaiya Adigalo." He sings this Thiruppathikam with the fervent desire to remove the obstruction and see the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru, and to fulfill Cheraman Perumal Nayanar's wish. He sings this glorious Thiruppathikam, calling out to the Lord with such desire. The greatness of this Thiruppathikam is that the Lord, who is the life of life and ever present, is called out as if He were far away, and the Lord responds accordingly. Calling out to the Lord, who is the life of life and ever present, as if He were far away, is the unique feature and greatness of this Thiruppathikam. The Thiruppathikam sung by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal for Thiruvaiyaru is the 77th Thiruppathikam in the Seventh Thirumurai, set to the tune of Kanthara Panchamam. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathikam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Paravum Parisu ondru ariyaen naan pande umai payilaathen iravum pagalum ninainthaalum eytha ninaiya maatten naan karavil aruvi kamugunna thengan kulaikkeezh karuppaalai aravam thirai kaveri kottatthu Aiyaru udaiya Adigalo." "I do not know how to praise You, I have not practiced with You before. Even if I think of You day and night, I cannot reach You. O Lord of Aiyaru, in the Cauvery region where waterfalls flow without end, where are areca palms and coconut trees, and where sugarcane grows, and where serpents reside." "Koodi adiyaar irunthaalum gunam ondru ileer kurippileer oodi irunthum unarkilen ummai thondan ooranaen thedi engum kaankilen thiruvarure sinthippan aadum thirai kaveri kottatthu Aiyaru udaiya Adigalo." "Even if devotees gather, You have no qualities, no marks. Even if I run, I cannot understand You, I, your servant Ooran. I search everywhere but cannot find You, I only think of Thiruvarur. O Lord of Aiyaru, in the Cauvery region with dancing waves." Thiruchitrambalam. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang the Thiruppathikam. The sixth and ninth verses are very special. "Kargol kondrai sadaimel ondru udaiyaay vidaiyaay nagaiyaal moorkar puram moonru eri seithaay munni pinni muthalvan nee vaar kol aruvi palavaari maniyum muthum ponnum kondu aarkkum thirai kaveri kottatthu Aiyaru udaiya Adigalo" is the sixth verse. "You, who have the konrai flower on your matted hair, who ride the bull, who burnt the three cities of the ignorant with a smile, You are the first and last. O Lord of Aiyaru, in the Cauvery region with roaring waves, where many waterfalls bring gems, pearls, and gold." "Kathirkol pasiyai otthaen naan kanden umai kaanaathen ethirthu neentha maatten emmaan thammaan thammaane virithu megam mazhai pozhiya vellam paranthu nurai sithari athirkkum thirai kaveri kottatthu Aiyaru udaiya Adigalo" is the ninth verse. "I am like the hungry sun, I saw You but did not see You. I cannot swim against You, my Lord, my Lord, my Lord. O Lord of Aiyaru, in the Cauvery region with roaring waves, where clouds pour rain, and the flood spreads and scatters foam." These verses serve as internal evidence for this history. As Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang the Thiruppathikam, the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru, hearing it, spoke "Olam" (a cry of distress) so that all animate and inanimate beings could hear. The 135th verse. The Lord, who is capable of dancing in the hall, in ancient Aiyaru, spoke "Olam" with a unified consciousness, so that all animate and inanimate beings could hear, like a mother cow crying out when its calf is obstructed and calls out, and the river Ponni (Cauvery) also showed the way by receding. The Lord, who is capable of dancing in the sacred hall, the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru, spoke "Olam" so that all animate and inanimate beings could hear, like a mother cow crying out when its calf is obstructed and calls out. If a calf is obstructed from approaching its mother cow, it will call out to its mother. Hearing that, the mother cow will also cry out, "Amma!" This is a natural phenomenon we see in the world. Here, Nambigal (Sundaramoorthy Swamigal) is like the calf, the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru is like the mother cow, and the Cauvery river is flowing like an obstruction between them. Therefore, for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who stood on the southern bank of the Cauvery and sang the Thiruppathikam, calling out to the Lord like a calf, the Lord of ancient Thiruvaiyaru, hearing that Thiruppathikam, spoke "Olam" with a unified mind, so that all animate and inanimate beings could hear. At that time, the Cauvery river also receded and showed the way. The 136th verse. The river, swelling to the sky, stood like a crystal mountain on the western side. The water on the eastern side receded, making a good path in the middle with cool sand spread. The devotees, seeing this, cried profusely and their hair stood on end, and they raised their hands in prayer. As soon as the sound of the Lord's words was heard, after Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang the Thiruppathikam, the river showed the way. It stopped flowing continuously and showed a path for walking. The Cauvery river, which had swelled to the sky, stood like a crystal mountain on the western side. Here, the Cauvery flows from west to east. So, the water coming from the west, that path, the western side of the Cauvery river, stood like a crystal mountain. On the eastern side, the remaining floodwaters of the river receded, and the river split in the middle, showing a path for walking. The water flow from the western side was dammed and stopped. The water on the eastern side receded. A sandbank formed in the middle, creating a walkway. Thus, the Cauvery river made way, spreading cool sand, without any obstacles like pebbles, for walking. All the devotees, seeing this, cried profusely like rain, and their hair stood on end, and they raised their hands in prayer above their heads. The 137th verse. Cheraman Peruman bowed to Nambi's feet. Nambiyarurar, the Lord of Navaloor, with a chest adorned with a golden sacred thread and gems, bowed in return to Cheraman Peruman and said, "Did not the Lord of the celestials grant this to you?" Both rejoiced and praised the Lord, and entered the middle of the great Cauvery. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar bows to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet. Seeing the grace that the Lord bestowed upon Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's hymn, and seeing that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, in accordance with his request, prayed to the Lord and found a way to go to Thiruvaiyaru and worship, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar bows to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Lord of Navaloor, with a chest adorned with a golden sacred thread and gems, bows in return to Cheraman Peruman Nayanar. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "Did not the Lord of the celestials grant this grace to you?" In response to Cheraman Peruman Nayanar's request to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, saying that his heart melts with desire to worship Thiruvaiyaru and asking to cross the river and worship, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a hymn, and the Lord granted it. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says to Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, "This grace was granted to you." Thus, both rejoiced together, praised the Lord, and entered the middle of the great Cauvery. The 138th verse. The great Tamil poet and the Chera king, with their retinue, walked on the sandy path granted by their Lord in the middle of the overflowing river. They crossed the river and reached the Lord of Pancha Nadi Vanam (Thiruvaiyaru), fell prostrate, rose, and praised Him.

They fell prostrate on the ground, rose, and praised Him. The 139th and 140th verses. Unable to contain the grace of Lord Shiva, they immersed themselves in it and worshipped. By the grace of their Lord, they bowed down again and came to the middle of the great Ponni river as before. As the water that stood like a lofty mountain receded and flowed again, they saw the wonder of this act. They praised the grace of the Lord of Aiyaru on the other bank, fell prostrate, rose, and proceeded westwards, worshipping other holy places of the Lord with the pure moon-adorned matted hair. They reached the distant Kongu country with the Chera king. These are the two verses that follow. Unable to fully comprehend the grace of the compassionate Lord, they immersed themselves in it and worshipped. They bowed before their Lord, the Lord of Thiruvaiyaru, and receiving His gracious permission, they came back to the middle of the great Cauvery river, on the same sandy path they had come before, and ascended the bank of the Cauvery. As soon as everyone ascended the southern bank of the Cauvery, the water that had stood like a great mountain until then quickly receded and flowed as before. Thus, these two great personages again came to the southern bank of the Cauvery. Witnessing the wonder of this gracious act, they praised the grace of the Lord residing in Thiruvaiyaru, from the southern bank. They fell prostrate on the ground, rose, and proceeded westwards on their pilgrimage. Continuing their pilgrimage along the southern bank of the Cauvery towards the west, they worshipped the temples where the Lord rejoices, such as Thiru Erumbiyur, Thiruchirappalli, Thirumukkeeswaram, and Thirukkadambanthurai. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar reached the Kongu country, the land of the righteous Kongars. Earlier, when Cheraman Perumal Nayanar undertook a pilgrimage from the mountain country to the Chola country, the kings and chieftains of the Kongu region had welcomed him without any shortcomings. Similarly, they welcomed him now. Hence, the Kongu country gained the glory of being called the righteous Kongu country. The 141st verse. Having crossed the Kongu country and reached the border of the glorious mountain country, our Lord's friend, Nambi, the Lord's friend, did not proceed further, but stood there with desire. All the people of that country rejoiced and came forward to welcome them. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar crossed the Kongu country. The path they took was the main road leading to Malayalam country via Karur, Kodumudi, and Avinashi. They crossed the Kongu country and reached the border of the mountain country. There, all the mountain people, hearing the news that Nambigal, the friend of their Chera king, the Lord's friend, was coming with their king, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, rejoiced greatly and prepared to welcome and praise both of them. They were very happy. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 25 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 961, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal proceed to the mountain country. The 142nd verse. Everywhere in the towns, there were arches; everywhere in the surroundings, there were flower gardens; everywhere on the paths, there were cool pandals; everywhere in the houses, there was the smoke of agarwood; everywhere in the rivers, there were heaps of flowers; everywhere in the vicinity, there were rising treasures of gems and gold; everywhere, there was the sound of drums; and everywhere on the ground, there was a shower of golden flowers. This verse describes how the people of the mountain country decorated and welcomed Cheraman Peruman Nayanar and Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. In every town, there were arches; everywhere in the surroundings, there were beautiful flower gardens; everywhere on the paths, there were cool pandals; everywhere in the houses, there was the smoke of agarwood rising like clouds; everywhere in the rivers, there were heaps of flowers; everywhere in the vicinity of those rivers, there were rising treasures of gems and gold washed ashore; everywhere, there was the sound of drums; and everywhere on the ground, there was a shower of bright golden flowers. Thus, they decorated. The 143rd verse. They reached Kodungolur, immersed in an endless flood of joy, seeing the great flood of ministers and armies coming from all directions, the flood of horses with manes, the flood of elephants with tusks, the flood of female elephants adorned with ornaments, and the flood of desired food. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Peruman Nayanar reached Kodungolur, immersed in an endless flood of joy, seeing the great flood of ministers and armies coming from all directions, the flood of horses with manes, the flood of elephants with tusks, the flood of female elephants adorned with ornaments, and the flood of desired food. Here, "mevum sotru vellam" (flood of desired food) refers to the custom of serving large quantities of food, which is still prevalent in the mountain country. The 144th verse. At the gate of Kodungolur, they adorned it with beautiful flags, decorated the streets, and arranged high gopurams, mansions, cool halls, platforms, long royal cities, and dance halls with continuous garlands of gems, areca nut flowers, and various other garlands, decorating them in different ways. At the gate of Kodungolur, they adorned it with decorations, decorated the streets with high gopurams that touched the stars, mansions, moonlit courtyards, cool halls, platforms, long royal cities, and dance halls. They arranged continuous garlands of gems, areca nut flowers, and various other garlands, decorating them in different ways. The 145th verse. As the city dwellers came forward to welcome them, they approached. In countless dance halls, women with fish-shaped earrings sang and danced. As they proceeded along the beautiful streets, the great Chera king, instead of going to his high mansion, took the incomparable devotee, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and entered Thiruvanchaikkalam. As the people of the city came forward to welcome them, they approached. In countless dance halls, women with fish-shaped earrings sang and danced. As they proceeded along the beautiful streets, the great Chera king, instead of going to his high mansion, took the incomparable devotee, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and entered Thiruvanchaikkalam. Even though Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, being a king, arrived at his capital city of Kodungolur, he did not go to his palace. Instead, he went to Thiruvanchaikkalam, the temple of his worshipped deity, which was about an hour's distance away. We see here that it is the custom of devotees, upon arriving at a holy place, to first worship in the temple before proceeding to the monastery. The 146th verse. They circumambulated the Lord's temple, bowed, and entered. With overflowing love, they fell prostrate and worshipped. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal stood before the Lord and began to sing the Thiruppathikam, starting with "Mudippathu Gangai," praising the Lord with the moon-crowned head, and worshipped with the Lord. They circumambulated the Lord's temple in Thiruvanchaikkalam, bowed, and entered. They stood directly before the Lord, fell prostrate with overflowing love, worshipped, and then stood before the Lord. Nambiyarurar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, then began to sing the Thiruppathikam in the proper manner. He began the Thiruppathikam with "Mudippathu Gangai." The divine Sekkizhar Peruman clearly indicates, through divine inspiration, that this Thiruppathikam was sung at this holy place. However, there are no specific mentions of Thiruvanchaikkalam in this Thiruppathikam. Nevertheless, due to divine inspiration, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman presents it to us in this way. Since there is no mention of the place name in this Thiruppathikam, it is included among the general Thiruppathikams, and it is also counted as the only Thiruppathikam for Thiruvanchaikkalam in the Thirumurai publications and research works on Thirumurai. But the divine Sekkizhar Peruman himself clearly states that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang this Thiruppathikam, which begins with "Mudippathu Gangai," before the Lord of Thiruvanchaikkalam. This Thiruppathikam, sung for Thiruvanchaikkalam, is the 44th Thiruppathikam in the Seventh Thirumurai, set to the tune of Kollikkavvanam. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathikam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Mudippathu Gangaiyum thingalum settathu mooveyil nodippathu maathirai neerilak kanai noorinaar. Kadippathum erum menranju van thirukkaigalal pidippathu paambandru illaiyo embiranukke." "He who wears the Ganga and the moon, who burnt the three cities in an instant with an arrow that did not lose its water. Is it not a serpent that He holds in His beautiful hands, which bite and rise, O my Lord?" "Kaichina maalvidai maanikatthu em karaikkandatthu eesanai ooran ettodu irandu virumbiya aayina seer pagai gnaniyap pannadi thondan thaan esina pesumin thondargal embiranaiye." "O devotees, speak of the Lord of the dark throat, who is like a ruby on a fierce bull, whom Ooran desired with eight and two. Speak of the servant who praised Him, the Lord." Thiruchitrambalam. We have contemplated the special significance of Thiruvanchaikkalam at the beginning of this Puranam. From this holy place, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar are about to proceed to Thirukkailayam. That day is the holy day of Adi month, Swathi star. This traditional festival was forgotten at this place for about six or seven centuries. Then, around 1935 CE, through the great efforts of the leader of the Coimbatore Sekkizhar Thirukkootam and our Periyapuranam commentator, Sivathiru Sivakavimani C.K. Subramania Mudaliar, with the permission of the Kochi king, the Adi Swathi festival is again celebrated there. To this day, this festival is celebrated annually with Gurupuja, Maheswara Puja, Thevaram recitation, Purana reading, and other events. By the order of the head of the Thiruppanandal Math, Kasiwasi Srimath Arunandi Thambiran Swamigal, endowments have been established, and for some years, Thevaram prizes, Maheswara Puja, and annadhanam (food offering) are held during the festival. Cheramanar Sundarar Sathiram has also been established in Thiruvanchaikkalam. The 147th verse. Having worshipped and immersed themselves, they came out. The king, with all the world praising him, first seated Nambi on the female elephant, then he himself mounted, and fanned him with flawless gem-studded chamaras. As they came to the golden mansion, the people on both sides of the street praised and glorified them. After worshipping the Lord and immersing themselves in that joy, they came out. Then, for the procession of Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, he first seated Nambigal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, on a well-decorated female elephant, so that the whole world would praise him. Then he himself mounted the elephant and came fanning him with beautiful chamaras (whisks), bringing him to his mansion. He first seated Sundaramoorthy Swamigal on the ambari (howdah) arranged for riding the elephant in procession, and then he himself mounted, fanning Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with a chamara from behind. We still see the custom of fanning with a chamara from behind the elephant in the mountain country. This act of welcome and worship performed by Cheraman Perumal Nayanar for Sundaramoorthy Swamigal was also performed by the Chola king Anapaya, who heard this history, for the author of this book, the divine Sekkizhar Peruman, as we see in the 77th verse of the Thiruthondar Purana Varalaru, composed by Srimath Umapathi Sivachariyar. "He placed the Thirumurai (sacred texts) on a golden vessel on the head of a fierce elephant, then seated the king's servants in order, and the king himself also mounted. As they came in procession along the street, with the sound of Vedic chants, and the celestials showered flowers, the king, remembering the Lord's grace, said, 'Is this not the fruit of my penance?'" This is the 77th verse in the Thiruthondar Purana Varalaru. Here, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar thus brings Sundaramoorthy Swamigal to his mansion. All the people who witnessed this scene praised them in various ways. The 148th verse. Some said, "He is a good friend to our king." Others said, "What boundless penance did we perform before to have him as a friend?" Some said, "What wealth will our mountain country now receive?" Others worshipped, saying, "Look at the deeds performed by Perumal, can they be described?" Some people who witnessed this scene said, "He is a good friend to our king, Cheraman Perumal." They praised Sundaramoorthy Swamigal as "Cheraman Thozhar" (friend of Cheraman). They praised him, understanding the superficial meaning that the king's hospitality was due to friendship. Now, we too have received the great fortune of worshipping him, considering that this act was not merely due to friendship, but a way of worshipping a devotee. They said that they received this boundless fortune due to the penance they performed before, praising it as the master-servant relationship. These people understood the subtle meaning. Now, some others praised, saying, "This is a great fortune not only for us but for this mountain country. What greater fortune can our mountain country have than this?" These people understood the extremely subtle meaning. Fanning Sundaramoorthy Swamigal from behind with a chamara, this act is fitting for all four paths of righteousness. Its greatness cannot be described. It is an act that culminates in the service of the Lord. They worshipped, saying, "Look at the deeds performed by our king Cheraman Perumal, can they be described?" They worshipped it as an instruction that showed the path of righteousness to the world. The 149th verse. They showered flowers, puffed rice, and golden dust, and worshipped. They marveled, saying, "This fertile country of Ponni, where he resides, is the tilak of the earth." The praises that spread everywhere came and reached the mansion. They entered the gem-studded gate, where horses and elephants were crowded, and dismounted. They showered flowers, puffed rice, and golden dust, and worshipped. They marveled, saying, "This fertile country of Ponni, where he resides, is the tilak of the earth." Thus, the praises that spread everywhere came and reached the mansion. Some praised, marveling that if their country became a country because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal came here, then the Chola country, where Sundaramoorthy Swamigal always resides, is what adorns the world. According to the Thevaram verse, "She forgot herself, heard his name, and became one with the feet of her Lord," these people, who praised in this way, forgot themselves and their country, and thought only of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and the country where he lived. Being deeply rooted in that state, they praised in this way. Thus, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar and his friend entered the gem-studded gate of the mansion, where horses and elephants were crowded, and dismounted from the elephant. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 26 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 962, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar arrive at Cheraman Perumal Nayanar's mansion. The 150th verse. The verse states that Kazharitrarivar, the Adigal, brought Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Lord of poets, and seated him on a shining throne in the glorious mansion, then stood before him. Kazharitrarivar, the Adigal, brought Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Lord of Arurar, and seated him on a shining throne in the glorious mansion. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar then stood before him. He honored Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's arrival as a greater festival than the Lord's own arrival. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is one who shines with both royal glory and Vedic glory. Therefore, accordingly, he was seated on a throne, and Cheraman Perumal Nayanar stood before him in reverence. The 151st verse. As the queens carried the fragrant water in golden vessels, and he began to wash his beautiful feet, Arurar, saying "This is not proper," withdrew his golden feet. He fell to the ground and said, "All that I do out of my love should be accepted here." Cheraman Perumal Nayanar intended to wash Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's beautiful golden feet with fragrant water from a golden vessel. Accordingly, his queens carried the fragrant water in golden vessels and poured it. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar began to wash Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's golden feet with that water. At that time, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal said, "This is not proper." Immediately, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar fell to the ground and requested, "You must accept all the worship I perform out of my love, without rejecting it." The 152nd verse. When Perumal requested, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was of great nobility in love, could not refuse. He witnessed all that the Uthiyar king with the long, beautiful shoulders did. After offering all the rare pujas in due order, he partook of the meal with the king who held the white umbrella like a full moon. When Cheraman Perumal Nayanar requested thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, unable to refuse, witnessed all that Cheraman Perumal Nayanar did. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, who was of great nobility in love, witnessed all that Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the king with the long, beautiful shoulders, did. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar performed all the rare and great pujas in due order. He performed all the pujas for Maheswara Puja, such as offering flowers, applying sandalwood paste, and lighting incense and lamps, according to the Shiva Agamas. The fact that these pujas were performed by a king, which is difficult for others to do, makes them worthy of Maheswara Puja. Nambigal, unable to refuse, only watched these pujas. After all these pujas were completed, he was delighted to partake of the meal with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar. We have seen in the puranas of Siruthonda Nayanar and many others that partaking of food with devotees brings great joy. The 153rd and 154th verses. After partaking of the divine meal with the Chera king, he offered fragrant sandalwood paste, musk paste, clothes, jeweled ornaments, cool, fragrant, soft flower garlands, and other such items to Vanthondar. He himself used the remaining items. Then, songs, dances, sweet musical instruments, and other pleasant entertainments for women were performed from time to time. The king, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, delighted Nambiyarurar, the Lord of Thirumunaippadi, by seating him in the fragrant flower gardens around his mansion. After partaking of the divine meal with Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, Cheraman Perumal Nayanar offered fragrant sandalwood paste, musk paste, clothes, jeweled ornaments, cool, fragrant, soft flower garlands, and other such items to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. He himself used the remaining items. First, he himself offered them to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, and then he used the remaining as his divine grace. Then, songs, dances, sweet musical instruments, and other pleasant and joyful entertainments for women were performed from time to time, honoring Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar, the king, delighted Nambiyarurar, the Lord of Thirumunaippadi, by seating him in the fragrant flower gardens around his mansion. The 155th verse. The king, with great desire, made him enjoy the joy of playing with a ball, the great joy of eating small rice dishes, the water sports in the flower-filled ponds where bees hum, and the elephant fights with tusks like pots, and wrestling. He made Sundaramoorthy Swamigal enjoy the games of playing with a ball. In addition, he offered the great joy of eating small rice dishes. Small rice dishes are various types of mixed rice dishes, which are served in small portions and eaten, hence the name "small rice dishes." Even though they are called small rice dishes, they bring great joy, so they are considered great and special. He then made him witness water sports in the flower-filled ponds where bees hum. He also made him witness elephant fights with tusks like pots, and wrestling matches performed by warriors. Thus, while Cheraman Perumal Nayanar was bringing joy to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal with these great deeds and great desire, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's memory of the Lord of Thiruvarur surged greatly. The 156th verse. One day, the Lord of poets, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, with his mind greatly remembering the feet of the Lord of Thiruvarur, the king of the city, sang the Thiruppathikam with the refrain "Can I forget Aruran, who is my very life?" and was greatly moved. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's mind was greatly filled with the memory of the feet of Thyagesa Peruman, the Lord who rules the city of Thiruvarur. Although he always had an uninterrupted memory of Thyagaraja Peruman, one day he had a very deep memory of him. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sang a Thiruppathikam with the refrain "Can I forget Aruran, who is my very life?" The meaning and refrain of the Thiruppathikam are "Can I forget Aruran, who is my very life?" Here, even though he was enjoying royal pleasures, and even though he was continuously practicing Shivayoga, he thought, "Can I forget Aruran? No, I cannot." Therefore, the intense desire to go to Thiruvarur surged in Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's mind. The Thiruppathikam sung by him in Thiruvanchaikkalam, remembering the Lord of Thiruvarur, is the 59th Thiruppathikam in the Seventh Thirumurai, set to the tune of Thakkesa Pann. The first and last verses of this Thiruppathikam are as follows: Thiruchitrambalam. "Ponnum meipporum tharuvaanai pogamum thiruvum punarppaanai pinnai en pizhai pizhaiyai poruppaanai pizhailellam thavira panippaanai inna thanmaiyan endru arivonna emmaan ezhivandha piranai annam vaigum vayal pazhanatthu ani aarooranai marakkalumaame." "Can I forget the Lord of beautiful Aaroor, in the fertile fields where swans reside, who gives gold and true wealth, who bestows pleasure and prosperity, who forgives my past mistakes, who makes me avoid all mistakes, my Lord whose nature cannot be known?" "Aaroor endru ulagangallukkellam uraikkalam porulaay udankoodi kaarurum kamizh kondrai nanmaalai mudiyan kaarigai kaaranamaaga aaroorai maratharkariyanaai ammaan than thirupper konda thondan aarooran adi naayurai vallar amaralogatthu iruppavar thaame." "Can I forget Aaroor, which is the meaning of all worlds, who is united with them, who wears a good garland of fragrant konrai flowers, who is difficult to forget because of the lady? Those who can speak the words of Aruran, the servant who bears the sacred name of the Lord, will reside in the celestial world." Thiruchitrambalam. This Thiruppathikam, though sung in Thiruvanchaikkalam remembering the Lord of Thiruvarur, is about Thiruvarur, so it is included among the Thiruppathikams of Thiruvarur. We have already contemplated that Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thiruppathikam "Pathimaiyum" was also sung from Thiruvottiyur for Thiruvarur and is included among the Thiruppathikams of Thiruvarur. This Thiruppathikam reveals the steadfastness of Nambigal's mind, who, even while enjoying royal wealth, remained in Shivayoga, continuously contemplating Thiruvarur. The depth and beauty of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's intense remembrance and melting devotion for the Lord of Thiruvarur in this Thiruppathikam are very much worth contemplating. It begins with "Ponnum meipporum" (gold and true wealth). Sundaramoorthy Swamigal shows that gold is different from true wealth. Here, "true wealth" refers to the knowledge of the Lord's feet. "Ponnum meipporum" then becomes "pogamum thiruvum" (pleasure and prosperity). These are arranged in order: pleasure comes from gold, and prosperity comes from the knowledge of the Lord's feet, which is true wealth. "Punarpavan" means one who bestows, knowing the measure, quality, and time. Thus, this Thiruppathikam is very moving. Thus, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, remembering Thiruvarur, sang the Thiruppathikam and decided to go and worship in Thiruvarur. Let us continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 27 — Kazharitrarivar Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 963, composed by the divine Sekkizhar Peruman. Varkonda Vanamulaiyal Sarukkam. Kazharitrarivar Nayanar Puranam. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal decides to remember Thiruvarur and go there to worship. The 157th verse. As he set out with his devoted followers, remembering Thiruvarur and intending to go and worship, the great king, with unceasing love, rose with him, bowed with folded hands, and, unable to bear the separation, followed him. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal sets out with his devoted followers, remembering Thiruvarur and intending to go there to worship. At that time, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, who had an inseparable friendship with him, with his heart melting due to that unceasing friendship, rose with him, bowed with folded hands, and, unable to bear the separation, followed Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 158th and 159th verses. Cheraman Peruman Nayanar went before Vanthondar, his mind filled with sorrow, and said, "Today I cannot bear your separation. What shall I do?" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal replied, "Do not grieve at all. Remain in your country and rule, crushing your enemies." When Arurar spoke thus, the gracious Chera king, hearing it, said, "For me, the rule of the earth and the heavens is your lotus feet. But you desired in your heart to go to Thiruvarur, with its long streets where chariots run. I fear to even explain that desire." Cheraman Peruman Nayanar went before Vanthondar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, his mind filled with sorrow, and said, "Today I cannot bear your separation. What shall I do?" Sundaramoorthy Swamigal replied, "Do not grieve at all. Remain in your country and rule, crushing all your enemies in battle." This rule refers to the rule of Shiva's wisdom. When Nambi Arurar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, spoke thus, the gracious Chera king, Kazharitrarivar Nayanar, hearing it, said, "For me, the rule of this earthly world and the heavenly world is your lotus feet. But you desired in your heart to go to Thiruvarur, with its long streets where chariots run. I fear to even explain that desire." Sundaramoorthy Swamigal commanded him to rule. But that rule is to serve Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet. Not only this worldly rule, but also the rule of wisdom, obtained by the indication of your command, which conquers internal enemies, is also to serve Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's feet. Even though he said this, he feared to explain Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's desire to go to Thiruvarur, where the Lord of the streets, the expert of the chariot, resides in a special way, as stated in the Thevaram, "I saw the Lord of the streets in Aaroor." Cheraman Peruman Nayanar says, "I, your servant, must always serve your lotus feet without separating from you. There are two ways for this. One is for me to follow you to Thiruvarur. But that was prevented by Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's command to remain in my country and rule. The other way is to request you to stay here. But it is also not proper to keep Sundaramoorthy Swamigal here, who has a heartfelt desire to go to Thiruvarur." Thus, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar speaks to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal. The 160th verse. When the king spoke thus, Vanthondar replied, "I, a thief with a strong heart, will not stay here, forgetting the beautiful Lord of Thiruvarur, who is dearer than my life. By the gracious grace of the Lord who wears the moon, you shall rule. You remain here," he bowed. When Cheraman Peruman Nayanar spoke thus, Vanthondar, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, replied to him.

"I, a thief with a strong heart, will not stay here, forgetting the beautiful Lord of Thiruvarur, who is dearer than my life. By the gracious grace of the Lord who wears the moon, you shall rule. You remain here," Sundaramoorthy Swamigal bowed. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal praises the Lord of Thiruvarur as "dearer than my life." The Lord is the life of life. Generally, the Lord is the prime mover who acts as the life of life for all beings. However, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal specifically refers to him as "my life" and "dearer than life," indicating his special relationship with the Lord. The Thevaram states, "He stood everywhere as the life of life." Even though the Prime Mover is pervasive everywhere, as stated in the 12th Sutra, 3rd Adhikarana of Sivagnanabodham, "He is manifest and stable like butter in curd only in two places, namely, the sacred form of the devotee and the Shiva temple, and in all other places, He is unmanifest like butter in milk." Therefore, by praising him as "dearer than my life," Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's intense love for the Lord of Thiruvarur is shown here. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "I, a thief with a strong heart, will not stay here, forgetting him." The meaning of this is that if he forgets the Lord of Thiruvarur, he would be a thief. Forgetting here means staying here without seeing and worshipping him directly. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal blesses him, saying, "You shall rule with the help of the boons granted to you by the Lord." This is because this rule was given to Cheraman Peruman Nayanar by the Lord. The Lord himself provided the necessary supporting factors and instruments for him to rule. Therefore, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal says, "You shall take up this rule given by the Lord and rule here." We are reminded here of the time when Thirunavukkarasu Peruman and Thirugnanasambandar Peruman met, and when Thirugnanasambandar Peruman tried to walk alongside Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, Thirunavukkarasar requested him to come in the pearl palanquin given to him by the Lord. We saw this in the 2425th verse, "The Nayanar, having been granted the auspicious and radiant pearl palanquin, you shall ascend it." The 161st verse. He too, bowing and agreeing, called his ministers and commanded them, "Bring all the great treasures, of various kinds, that have accumulated in this beautiful ancient city until today, loaded on many people." After hearing these replies from Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the king, Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, bowed to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and agreed. He then called all his ministers and commanded them, "Bring all the great treasures of various kinds that have accumulated in this beautiful ancient city until today, loaded on many people." "Bandaram" refers to the treasures in the treasury. The 162nd and 163rd verses. As commanded by Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, the ministers gathered all the accumulated wealth, precious gold, nine gems, shining jeweled ornaments, various types of clothes, and various types of fragrant ingredients. They made them into large bundles and loaded them on the people, bringing them until the ground was covered. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, the Lord of Thirumunaippadi, having received all these from his attendants, bowed to the golden feet of Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, and then embraced him, whose shoulders were like a mountain, and bid him farewell. As commanded by Cheraman Peruman Nayanar, the ministers gathered all the accumulated wealth until that day, precious gold, nine gems, shining jeweled ornaments, various types of clothes, and various types of fragrant ingredients. They made them into large bundles and loaded them on the people, bringing them until the ground was covered.
